**Satellite findings narrative fixed (project owner directive, outside the corridor-testing cadence).** The owner flagged that the satellite section's narrative "needs work" -- investigation found the "The Kacific gap" paragraph, hardcoded identically in both `ascii_report.py` and `html_report.py`, claimed "no traceroute has ever touched Kacific's network" and "AS38201 sits untested" -- both now false, since AS135409<->AS38201 has been traceroute-confirmed twice (FJ, then NC, two and one tranches ago respectively). The paragraph was static prose, never wired to the underlying data, so it silently went stale the moment those confirmations landed.

A second, deeper problem surfaced during the fix: `SatellitePathway.traceroute_confirmed_economies` conflated two different things -- for Kacific it read `('FJ', 'NC', 'TO')`, but FJ and NC were just the traceroute *source* economies (vantage points), not economies Kacific actually serves; only TO (Tonga) is the real customer-side relationship. `_compute_satellite_pathways` was folding `detour.source_cc` into the same set as `detour.target_cc`.

**Fix applied in `reports/data.py`:**
- `SatellitePathway` gained a new field, `traceroute_vantage_economies`, cleanly separated from `traceroute_confirmed_economies` -- the former is "tested from here" (source_cc/vantage_point_cc), the latter is "this economy's own reachability depends on this operator" (target_cc/customer_cc only). Docstring rewritten to explain the distinction and to stop asserting Kacific-specific facts as permanent.
- `_compute_satellite_pathways` reworked to put `target_cc` (ConfirmedDetour), `customer_cc` (ConfirmedLocalTransit), and `target_cc` (CandidatePeering) into the confirmed/candidate sets, and `source_cc`/`vantage_point_cc` into the new vantage set -- no more mixing.
- New `describe_satellite_pathway(sat, economy_names)` function: generates one plain-text paragraph per operator, computed fresh from current data every call -- names which economies are confirmed-served, how many vantage points tested it, and which RIS-observed relationships remain genuinely untested by any traceroute. Cannot go stale the way the hardcoded blurb did, since it reads live counts rather than asserting a fixed narrative.
- `ReportData` gained `satellite_narrative: tuple[str, ...]`, computed once in `build_report_data()` and consumed identically by both renderers (no format-specific logic duplicated).
- Both renderers: replaced the static "Kacific gap" callout with a loop over `data.satellite_narrative`; added a "Tested from (vantage points)" column to the satellite table in both formats, alongside the existing RIS/confirmed-served columns.

**Verified**: all three touched modules (`reports/data.py`, `reports/ascii_report.py`, `reports/html_report.py`) import cleanly. Regenerated all three artifacts (`report.txt`, `report.html`, `geographic_detours.svg`) -- no breakage, `pacific-peering-viz-detours` (which imports from `reports/data.py` indirectly) still runs clean. Spot-checked the actual rendered output in both formats: narrative now correctly shows Starlink/SES Astra/Kacific each with accurate served vs. vantage vs. still-untested economies (e.g. SES Astra: served NR only, tested from 5 vantage points including CK/KI/PG which are themselves listed as still-untested on the *served* side -- exactly the source/target distinction this fix was for). No corridor testing was done in this entry -- it's a standalone report-generation fix requested directly by the project owner, not a `/loop` tranche.

**Bulk NU(AS55885) clear-out, project owner directive.** Owner asked to run the entire remaining Niue queue (109 candidates) in one go rather than the usual 5-corridor cadence. Investigation confirmed AS55885 has only one connected probe (`64391`, per `data/atlas/asn_probe_registry.json`), so its measurements queue and run one at a time -- firing more at once doesn't speed anything up for that source alone. **The owner then made the key efficiency observation**: parallelism comes from spreading work across *different* source-economy probes, not from batch size on one probe, since each corridor's source ASN is fixed to its own physical probe(s) and different sources' queues don't share resources. Acted on this: fired the remaining backlog for every source with unused capacity at once -- all 109 NU candidates (single probe, will serialize), plus the full FM (AS139759, 3 probes, 29 candidates), GU (AS152735, 3 probes, 4 candidates), and NC (AS45345 + AS56089, 15 candidates) queues -- 157 measurements total, i.e. the entire current backlog, fired together so the multi-probe sources drain quickly in parallel while Niue's single-probe queue grinds in the background rather than being left idle waiting for its own dedicated tranche. Consolidated tracking onto one persistent Monitor polling all 157 IDs at once (superseded two earlier, narrower monitors). Results being processed in batches as they complete rather than waiting for all 157 -- see subsequent tranche entries below for what's actually been classified and filed.

**Deferred: build a real per-source-ASN measurement scheduler (not started).** Prompted by the same owner observation above, generalized: over a week-scale grind through the rest of the backlog, this project would benefit from an actual scheduler module (e.g. `atlas/scheduler.py`) instead of the current manual batch-and-refire pattern. Concept: partition the backlog by source ASN (each is an independent queue, since corridors can't be reassigned to a different source's probes); cap each queue's in-flight count at that ASN's connected-probe count (1 for Niue-like single-probe economies, higher for others); poll measurement status on an interval and immediately fire the next queued corridor the instant a slot frees, so every probe stays saturated without a human/agent re-deriving remaining candidates and re-polling each session. Would run as a persistent background loop (or a `/schedule` cron job) rather than inside an interactive tranche.

**RESOLVED (Phase 7)**: built for real as `auto_classify.run_batch()` -- one source ASN in flight per worker thread (exactly the "partition by source ASN, cap in-flight" concept above), driven by real system cron (`scripts/nightly_corridor_testing.sh`, Mon-Sat 1am) rather than an interactive tranche. Not a literal per-ASN poll-and-refill queue (it's a fixed pool of worker threads re-picking after each completion, not one persistent queue per ASN), but delivers the same practical outcome -- every available source ASN stays saturated for the whole time-boxed window without a human re-deriving remaining candidates each session.

**Also flagged, not yet designed: probe churn.** The owner separately raised that probes come online and go offline over time (RIPE Atlas hosts are volunteer-run home/business connections, not a fixed fleet) -- any scheduler built per the above needs to handle this, not just read `asn_probe_registry.json` once and assume it's static. Open design questions for whoever picks this up: (1) how often to refresh the registry (`build_asn_probe_registry()` already exists and re-queries Atlas's own API per economy -- could run on a cron alongside the scheduler, or be triggered by repeated `No suitable probes` measurement failures as a low-cost signal that a source's probe roster changed); (2) what to do with a source ASN that loses its only probe mid-run (its queue should pause, not error, and its already-`Scheduled`-but-not-yet-`Ongoing` measurements may need to be tracked for eventual failure rather than assumed successful); (3) what to do when a *new* probe comes online for a previously probe-less in-scope ASN (the corridor backlog's `enumerate_candidate_corridors` already filters to "source has a connected probe" -- a fresh probe should make previously-excluded source ASNs newly eligible, which argues for periodic backlog regeneration, not just periodic probe-registry refresh, so newly-testable corridors actually surface). None of this is designed in detail yet -- recorded here so it isn't lost, not acted on.

**MOSTLY RESOLVED (Phases 7-8)**, worth closing out explicitly: (1) registry refresh cadence -- `weekly_discovery_refresh.sh` calls the full pipeline (including `build_asn_probe_registry()`) every Sunday, so the registry is no longer read-once-and-assumed-static; (2) a source ASN failing mid-run -- `run_batch()`'s per-corridor `try/except` already catches this without crashing the worker, and simply leaves that corridor untested for a future pull (self-healing, matches the project's existing pattern of only calling `mark_corridor_tested()` after a corridor actually completes); (3) a newly-connected probe making previously-excluded source ASNs eligible -- already true today, since `enumerate_candidate_corridors()` reads the probe registry fresh every call and the weekly job regenerates the backlog right after refreshing that registry. **Still genuinely open**: nothing currently distinguishes "probe never had this ASN" from "probe just dropped off mid-week" for alerting purposes -- a probe that goes offline mid-week simply stops producing candidates silently until the next Sunday refresh, with no explicit notice. Low priority given how rarely this project's own probe coverage has actually changed, but a real, undesigned gap if someone wants proactive notice rather than silent adaptation.

**Terminology fix, project owner directive**: "global transit, not a named exchange crossing" -> "international transit, not a named exchange crossing" throughout `detour_ix_name` field values (65 instances across 127 `ConfirmedDetour` entries). Scoped specifically to that labeled pattern -- a handful of standalone narrative uses of "global transit" in note prose elsewhere were deliberately left alone since they weren't part of the pattern the owner referenced. Caught a real bug applying this: a first-pass line-by-line replace missed 14 instances where the words "global" and "transit" were split across two concatenated Python string literals (`"...-- global "` / `"transit, not a..."`), an artifact of how these fields are hand-wrapped in the source. Fixed with a second, more careful pass; verified zero instances of the old phrase remain in the regenerated `report.txt`/`report.html`. Committed standalone (`82c9f34`), not bundled with corridor-tranche work.

**Follow-up terminology simplification, same owner directive, same turn**: owner then asked to drop ", not a named exchange crossing" entirely, not just reword it -- every `detour_ix_name` now ends cleanly at "... -- international transit" (or "... -- satellite operator, international transit" for the two satellite-operator entries), no trailing qualifier clause. Used a proper multi-line-aware regex this time (join each field's concatenated string pieces, strip the trailing phrase, rewrite as one clean string) rather than another fragile line-by-line pass, precisely because the line-splitting bug just above showed that approach breaks silently. All 127 entries verified importing cleanly; regenerated and grepped all three output artifacts for zero remaining instances of the phrase before committing.

**Deferred: probe-to-probe geolocation anchoring (not started).** Project owner's observation: RIPE Atlas probes carry verified lat/lon metadata (`/api/v2/probes/{id}/`), unlike intermediate router hops, which this project has only ever geolocated indirectly (hostname PTR parsing via `hop_geolocation.py`, PeeringDB facility lookups, IXP LAN prefix matching -- all manual, all this session). Firing traceroutes *between* two known Atlas probes (rather than probe-to-arbitrary-target-IP) would give real RTT-vs-known-distance anchor points specific to this region's actual undersea/terrestrial cable paths, which could then (a) sanity-check existing hop-location guesses elsewhere, since a guessed location implying a wildly inconsistent RTT is a red flag, or (b) independently corroborate an already-filed detour-hub finding, since a probe-to-probe RTT matching the expected round-trip through a named hub is a second confirmation angle beyond hostname/facility evidence. Constraint worth noting: many in-scope economies have only 0-1 connected probes (Niue: exactly 1, observed directly this session), so the number of usable probe-to-probe pairs for anchoring is limited, though even a small number would help given how non-obvious real Pacific cable-routing RTT profiles are (a "close" island pair can route through a much more distant hub, which is itself the kind of finding this project already looks for, not noise to correct out). Not designed or built -- recorded here alongside the scheduler and probe-churn items above.

**Bulk clear-out, NU(AS55885) final sub-batch (28 corridors) -- corridor backlog fully cleared.** All 3/3 (actually 1/1, single-probe) traceroutes returned clean, no loops flagged on any of the 28.

Filed 16 new `ConfirmedDetour` entries, seven genuinely first-ever confirmations: AS45495(Interchange Ltd)<->AS132228 and <->AS132254 (siblings, identical RIS count 670); AS45355(Digicel Fiji)<->AS132429 (its *only* RIS neighbor, 1,316 of 1,316); AS45495<->AS133383 (its only neighbor too); AS136557<->AS136996 (a genuinely new intermediate carrier for this project); and -- notably -- AS139609(SISCC)<->AS142279 and <->AS150349, both targets whose FM-sourced attempts two batches ago dead-ended one hop short and were logged inconclusive, now confirmed directly by this second attempt reaching one hop further. 6 `ConfirmedLocalTransit` extensions (AS132486/AS154100 downstream-chain-and-direct at 14, AS134783 at 13, AS45891/SISCC-sibling at 12, AS55722 at 10, AS58932 and AS133897 at 5 each). 4 new `CandidatePeering` entries, all repeating the exact one-hop-off pattern already found via FM for the same three targets (132462, 139277, 150403), plus one new one (132797, real relationship AS133383 -- itself newly confirmed this same batch). 1 logged inconclusive (AS140504/SES Astra, the same well-known first-address dead-end pattern, still not retried).

**Real bug caught and fixed before this entry was written, not after**: the first write-up pass mismatched several measurement IDs against their actual targets -- copy-transcription errors from working off a mental summary instead of the verified `asn -> measurement_id` mapping. Caught by re-deriving that mapping fresh from the saved batch metadata and diffing it against what had already been written, *before* running `mark_corridor_tested()` or committing. Six IDs were wrong (AS132468, AS132486, AS134783, AS136996, AS139609, AS142279, AS150349 -- one swap between AS132486 and AS134783's entries was the same error appearing in two places) and all six were corrected in place. This closes out the entire 157-corridor bulk clear-out that began with the project owner's "do the entire NU queue at once" directive several tranches ago, which itself grew from 109 (Niue alone) to 157 once the parallel-across-probes insight extended it to every source with unused capacity (FM, GU, NC) at the same time. Remaining candidate space is not literally exhausted forever -- it will grow again as new probes come online (the deferred probe-churn item above is directly relevant) or as new RIS relationships appear (the standing 8-hourly backlog-regeneration job already watches for both) -- but there is, right now, no untested corridor left to pull.

**Phase 6: move off LLM classification, SQLite findings store + `auto_classify.py`.** Project owner's explicit direction with the backlog freshly empty: "the overall goal now is to move off LLM processing and get UV scripts doing all of the work." Everything below was built and verified without further pauses for confirmation, per the owner's own "run this as long as you can without supervision, I will be checking in later."

**SQLite migration (`analysis/store.py`, `analysis/migrate_legacy_findings.py`).** The three hand-written dataclass modules (`confirmed_detours.py`, `confirmed_local_transit.py`, `candidate_peering.py`) are now frozen historical snapshots -- read once by the migration script, no longer live-imported anywhere. New schema: one `findings` table (the adjacency + classification) plus one `corroborations` table (one row per traceroute that touched it) -- a single logical model for all three legacy shapes, which on inspection were never structurally different, only different presentation conventions (`ConfirmedDetour` gave each source economy its own entry; the other two gave the adjacency one entry with prose appended per corroboration). Two-layer storage, mirroring how `report.txt`/`report.html` are already treated: `data/analysis/findings.db` (gitignored, live working store) + `findings_export.jsonl` (repo root, committed, one finding per line, `sort_keys=True` for a stable diff) -- `import_jsonl()` rebuilds the database from the committed export on a fresh clone.

Migration surfaced and fixed real data-quality bugs the legacy convention had been silently carrying, not just reformatted around them: the original `UNIQUE(kind, source_asn, target_asn)` schema draft failed outright on `ConfirmedDetour` (never tracked a literal source ASN, only `source_cc` -- fixed by making `source_asn` nullable and keying the constraint on `source_cc` instead) and on genuine accidental duplicates in the legacy corpus (most dramatically 8 separate `CandidatePeering` entries for the same GU/AS38875-target relationship, artifacts of the old "new entry per source economy" convention bleeding into a shape that was never supposed to work that way) -- fixed with merge logic that folds duplicate `(kind, source_cc/asn, target_asn)` keys into one finding with multiple corroboration rows, appending each duplicate's legacy note rather than picking one arbitrarily and discarding the other.

Migration result, verified: 188 unique findings (157 `confirmed_detour`, 13 `confirmed_local_transit`, 18 `candidate_peering` -- 18 not 27 because the GU/AS38875 dedup alone folded 8 entries into 1), 198 total corroborations. Full end-to-end regeneration (all 5 report/viz/backlog commands) diffed clean against pre-migration output -- the only diffs were row reordering (new query orders by `kind, target_asn, source_cc`; legacy files were in original insertion order) and the expected count deltas from the intentional dedup merges, both confirmed via `grep -c`, not just assumed from a clean exit code.

**Real bug caught during migration, separate from the schema issues above**: `corridor_backlog.py`, `viz/geographic.py`, and `reports/probe_gap_report.py` were still importing directly from the three legacy `.py` files independently of `reports/data.py`'s store-backed rewrite -- meaning any finding `auto_classify.py` writes going forward would have been invisible to the backlog's `_tested_pairs_from_findings()` (re-offering already-classified corridors) and to the map/probe-gap reports. Found via `grep -rln` for the old import pattern across `src/`, fixed identically in all three (module-level `_store.connect()` + `load_confirmed_detours()`/`load_confirmed_local_transit()`/`load_candidate_peering()` + `_conn.close()`, same pattern `reports/data.py` already used).

**`known_anomalies.py`**: CIDR-keyed registry (`KnownLoopLocation`, `classify_loop_address()`) of every routing-loop location this project individually investigated and judged ordinary this session -- Equinix Sydney's IXP fabric, Superloop's own network, SISCC's own space, Starlink's own network, a 2-node loop inside AS174/Cogent, BNL Tarawa's own space. Seeded with 6 entries, verified against 6 real historical addresses (all matched) plus a deliberate non-match (`8.8.8.8`). This is what lets `auto_classify.py` tell a routine, already-understood loop apart from a genuine new anomaly worth escalating -- growth path is additive (add an entry whenever an escalation gets investigated and resolved as ordinary), not a one-time seed.

**`auto_classify.py`**: the actual end-to-end pipeline, registered as `pacific-peering-auto-classify`. Per corridor: `pick_next_corridor()` -> fire via `atlas.smoketest.run_smoketest()` (reused as-is -- it already does fire+poll+persist raw+parsed) -> `analyze_measurement()` for RIS/Atlas triangulation -> classify via a fixed mechanical decision tree, in priority order: (1) loop check with the real target IP against `known_anomalies` -- a recognized location is routine, an unrecognized one escalates; (2) an IXP crossing that's classified out-of-fishbowl (`in_fishbowl is False`, never the ungoverned `"TBA"`) at a hub this project has coordinates for (`economy_coordinates.EXTERNAL_HUB_LATLON`) -> Confirmed Detour; (3) RIS+Atlas agreement on the target's immediate upstream, where that upstream is itself a fishbowl ASN (`asn_registry.json`) -> Confirmed Local Transit; (4) real, contiguous signal RIS doesn't independently confirm -> Candidate Peering; (5) nothing real reached at all -> retry once against the target ASN's next cached prefix (the "a dead end can be one address's property, not the whole ASN" lesson from earlier this project, not re-derived from scratch), still dark after that -> Inconclusive, marked tested, no finding filed. New-vs-extend decided via `store.find_existing`/`find_existing_detour` before every `create_finding`. Every outcome -- including escalations -- calls `mark_corridor_tested()`; artifact regeneration (`findings_export.jsonl`, corridor backlog, both reports, the geographic map) runs as subprocesses after every corridor, not in-process, because `reports/data.py`/`corridor_backlog.py` read their SQLite-backed constants at import time and a long-lived classifier process can't just re-import them mid-run.

Escalations write to a new committed file, `escalations.md` (repo root, same convention as `task_plan.md`/`CHANGELOG.md`/`corridor_backlog.md` -- append-only, human-readable, git-tracked) rather than being logged and dropped, per the owner's "flag only real anomalies" direction and the standing "consult Terry on anomalies" practice from earlier in this project.

**Validated against real historical data before any live firing**, since the backlog is currently empty and there was nothing to test live without fabricating a corridor: re-ran the classification decision tree's core logic (not `classify_corridor()` itself, to avoid mutating the real database) against two already-triangulated measurements. GU->PG/AS17828 (measurement 210901499, the project's very first confirmed detour): upstream AS6939 (Hurricane Electric), `ris_agrees=True`, IXP crossing = Equinix Sydney, `in_fishbowl=False`, city=Sydney, mapped hub -- reproduces `confirmed_detour` with `detour_ix_name`/`detour_hub` exactly matching the real filed entry. FM->PW/AS17893 (measurement 210986430, the project's first candidate peering): upstream AS139759, `ris_agrees=False` on all 3 probes, an in-fishbowl Guam IX crossing that correctly does *not* trigger the detour branch -- reproduces `candidate_peering` with `probe_agreement="3/3 probes"`, exactly matching the real filed entry.

`bgp_tools.fetch_asn_names` (already existed, built for a different purpose -- bgp.tools corroboration) reused as-is for auto-populating `source_name`/`target_name`/upstream holder names on new findings, distinct from `CorridorCandidate.source_name`/`target_name` (which are *economy* names from `fishbowl.json`, not ASN operator names).

New entry points registered in `pyproject.toml`: `pacific-peering-findings-export` (`store.py:main`, previously built but never wired up) and `pacific-peering-auto-classify` (`auto_classify.py:main`).

Not yet done: a header note on the three frozen legacy `.py` files documenting their new status (functionally frozen already, just undocumented at the top of the file); committing this entire phase (SQLite migration + classifier) -- nothing from Phase 6 has been committed yet as of this entry. The classifier is untested against a live corridor (backlog is empty) -- first real validation happens whenever a new probe or RIS relationship next appears and the standing 8-hourly regeneration job surfaces a fresh candidate.

**Phase 7: move both standing loops off Claude's own session-scoped cron, onto real system cron.** Two session-only `CronCreate` jobs had accumulated by this point -- an 8-hourly corridor-backlog regen that turned out to only re-diff against whatever registries already happened to be on disk (never actually re-pulling APNIC/RIS/PeeringDB/IRR itself), and a 10-minute "pull the next corridor and test it" job, both of which die the moment the session ends. Project owner's direction, in two parts: (1) full discovery refresh moves to a real system cron, once a week, Sunday 1am -- "I'm not expecting more changes than that"; (2) corridor testing moves to a real system cron too, Monday-Saturday 1am, "fitting as many tests as possible into 2 hours."

**`scripts/weekly_discovery_refresh.sh`** -- `uv run pacific-peering-pipeline` (APNIC/RIS/IXP-LAN/IRR/probe-registry, free APIs, no LLM, no Atlas credits) + `pacific-peering-corridor-backlog`, then commits+pushes `corridor_backlog.md`/`outputs/runs/*` if either changed (scoped `git add`, not `-A`, so an unattended job can never sweep in unrelated in-progress work). Installed as `0 1 * * 0` in the real system crontab (`crontab -l`, confirmed `Australia/Brisbane` local time matches the requested 1am). Smoke-tested for real before trusting it unattended: full run succeeded, hit the same pre-existing PeeringDB rate-limit on `ix_id=716` the manual pipeline runs already handle gracefully, wrote and pushed a commit (`0d57107`).

**Built the actual concurrent scheduler this project had deferred earlier this session** (the "per-source-ASN measurement scheduler" item from the corridor-parallelization discussion): `auto_classify.run_batch(time_budget_seconds, max_concurrent=5)`. Real design, not a toy -- applies the parallel-across-probes insight for real: a single source ASN's connected probe serializes its own measurements no matter how fast work is submitted to it, so actual throughput comes from running several *different* source ASNs' measurements concurrently, never from batching more work against one. Each worker thread holds exactly one source ASN in flight at a time (`in_flight: set[int]`, checked-and-claimed under a lock before firing); `classify_corridor()` was refactored to accept an optional `lock` -- when given, only the fast local writes (SQLite, `tested_pairs.json`, `escalations.md`) are serialized under it, while firing a measurement and waiting for Atlas (the genuinely slow part, and the only reason concurrency helps at all) runs unlocked. A per-corridor exception is caught and logged, not allowed to silently kill a worker thread -- the corridor just stays untested and gets picked up by a future pull, matching the project's existing self-healing pattern (`mark_corridor_tested` only runs after a corridor actually completes). Stops pulling *new* work once the time budget passes; anything already in flight is allowed to finish, since a fired Atlas measurement can't be un-fired. Artifact regeneration moved from per-corridor (the existing single-shot `classify_corridor`/`classify_next` path, unchanged) to once at the end of the whole batch (`regenerate=False` passed internally) -- doing it after every corridor in a tight 2-hour window would spend the budget on subprocess overhead, not testing.

New CLI: `pacific-peering-auto-classify-batch [--hours H] [--max-concurrent N]` (`auto_classify.py:main_batch`), defaults `--hours 2 --max-concurrent 5`. Dry-run tested against the still-empty backlog: all worker threads correctly return immediately with zero busy-waiting (confirmed: 5 concurrent workers, 10ms total elapsed, 0 corridors -- no hang, no spin).

**`scripts/nightly_corridor_testing.sh`** -- wraps the batch command, then commits+pushes `findings_export.jsonl`/`corridor_backlog.md`/`outputs/reports`/`outputs/viz`/`escalations.md` if anything changed (`escalations.md` only added to the commit if it actually exists -- `git add` errors on a path never created, and no escalation may have happened that night). Installed as `0 1 * * 1-6` (Monday-Saturday, deliberately skipping Sunday so it never races the discovery refresh at the same hour). Smoke-tested for real: exits cleanly with "nothing new was testable" against the empty backlog, no false commit.

Retired both of the old session-scoped `CronCreate` jobs (the 8-hourly backlog-only regen and the 10-minute pull-and-test) now that real system cron covers both purposes properly. Updated `CLAUDE.md`'s session-start section to point at the two scripts instead of describing manual steps to re-derive by hand every session.

Not yet validated: a genuine live concurrent batch run (backlog has been empty throughout this phase) -- first real test happens automatically this coming Monday-Saturday 1am once the Sunday discovery refresh has had a chance to surface something.

---

**Phase 8: rolling reverification -- "how often do we recheck all corridors?"** Project owner's question, answered and then built the same turn. A weekly full re-check was rejected on the reasoning that physical/contractual routing relationships in this region (cable paths, IXP memberships, transit contracts) don't move week to week, so testing all 188 findings that often would mostly burn Atlas credits for zero new information; monthly is proportionate to the actual rate of change. Refined per the owner's own follow-up ("just set up the oldest 1/4 for retesting that week") into a *rolling* design rather than one giant monthly burst: the weekly Sunday job stages the oldest-verified quarter every week, so a full rotation happens roughly every 4 weeks, spread across the week's nightly runs instead of competing with new-corridor testing for a whole night's budget.

**Real architectural wrinkle discovered while designing this, not while building it**: the existing backlog system (`enumerate_candidate_corridors()`) permanently excludes an *economy pair* the moment any finding exists for it (`_tested_economy_pairs_from_findings()`), by design, from way earlier this session ("move to a new economy pair once one is well-established, rather than exhaustively testing every ASN combination"). That's exactly right for *new-corridor discovery* but fundamentally incompatible with *reverification* -- a finding that already exists is precisely what needs to flow back through for a fresh look, and the economy-pair dedup would silently block it forever. Solved by **not** routing reverification through `enumerate_candidate_corridors()`/`pick_next_corridor()` at all -- it's a structurally separate mechanism (a queue of specific finding IDs, not a backlog of untested pairs) that happens to feed into the same `classify_corridor()` firing/classification logic, reusing it unchanged.

**New in `auto_classify.py`**: `select_reverification_batch(fraction=0.25)` ranks all findings by their most recent corroboration's timestamp (oldest first) and returns the oldest quarter's IDs. `write_reverification_queue()` persists that to `data/analysis/reverify_queue.json` (gitignored, weekly-overwritten -- "this week's ration," not an ever-growing backlog; anything not gotten to stays near the front for next time regardless, since it's still among the oldest-verified). `_candidate_from_finding()` rebuilds a firable `CorridorCandidate` from an existing finding by using its most recent corroboration's vantage point as the source and the finding's own target -- literally re-running the test that originally produced the finding, not searching for something new. `_pop_reverify_candidate()` is the concurrency-safe queue-drain, called under `run_batch()`'s existing write lock, that skips (but doesn't discard) any queued finding whose vantage-point ASN is currently in flight in another worker.

**Real data bug caught immediately by testing against the live 188-finding database, not assumed**: every one of the ~157 detour findings migrated from the legacy `ConfirmedDetour` dataclass has `corroborations.vantage_point_asn = NULL` -- the migration script never set it (only `vantage_point_cc`), because the legacy shape never tracked a literal source ASN. `_candidate_from_finding()`'s first version required `vantage_point_asn` and would have silently dropped every single legacy finding from ever being reverifiable, permanently. Fixed with `_any_probe_asn_for_cc()`, a fallback that picks any currently-connected probe ASN in the vantage economy -- correct because firing is always country-based (`run_smoketest`'s `source_cc`), never actually keyed by the literal source ASN; the ASN field only matters for bookkeeping/display. Verified directly against 5 real findings from the live database (49, 19, 114, 53, 24) -- all five correctly reconstructed valid, real `CorridorCandidate`s (AS3605/GU, AS45345/NC, AS24390/FJ, all matching their real economies).

**Concurrency-safety verified with a targeted test, not just reasoning about it**: found two real findings (IDs 1, 12) sharing the same vantage-point source ASN, staged them as a 2-item test queue, and confirmed `_pop_reverify_candidate()` correctly returns `None` (rather than incorrectly returning either one) when that shared ASN is passed as excluded, and leaves the queue file completely untouched -- the exact "everything left is blocked" case the nightly worker loop needs to handle correctly to avoid either a duplicate-source race or silently losing a queued item.

`weekly_discovery_refresh.sh` now runs `pacific-peering-reverify-enqueue` as a third step (after pipeline + backlog regen); `nightly_corridor_testing.sh`'s header comment updated to describe draining two sources (reverification queue first, then new corridors). New CLI: `pacific-peering-reverify-enqueue [--fraction F]` (`auto_classify.py:main_reverify_enqueue`).

**Live end-to-end smoke test of the updated weekly script** (not just the new pieces in isolation): full run against the real 188-finding database staged exactly 47 findings (`ceil(188 * 0.25)`), committed and pushed cleanly (`b2544f2`). Not yet validated: the nightly job actually draining this queue and firing real reverification measurements -- first real test is this coming Monday 1am.

**Current open-items list** (as of this entry -- superseding scattered "not yet done"/"deferred" notes elsewhere for anything covered here; those entries stay as historical record, this is the live summary):

- **Full code review of Phases 6-8, done (2026-09-17).** Dispatched a reviewer subagent against `4b942fd..d1797ec` (the SQLite migration, `auto_classify.py`'s classification/batch/reverification logic, `known_anomalies.py`, `migrate_legacy_findings.py`, both unattended cron scripts). Concurrency design (the highest-risk area -- `run_batch()`'s lock scoping, `in_flight` tracking, the reverify-queue-first pick order) held up: verified sound, no TOCTOU window, no double-fire risk. Two real issues found, both hand-verified against the actual code (not just trusted from the subagent's report):
  - **Critical, fixed same day**: `classify_corridor()`'s three success-path branches (`confirmed_detour`/`confirmed_local_transit`/`candidate_peering`) returned without ever calling `_write_escalations()` -- only the fallthrough (`escalated`/`inconclusive`) path wrote them. Any loop/IXP/upstream anomaly detected on a corridor that *also* resolved to a filed finding was silently dropped, never reaching the git-tracked `escalations.md`, defeating the whole point of the escalation mechanism for exactly the corridors most likely to need it. Fixed by writing escalations at each of the three success-branch returns too (`auto_classify.py`), verified by import + syntax check; no behavior change to the classification logic itself, purely restores the intended write path.
  - **Important, deferred (owner's call, per-ASN identity confirmed)**: `find_existing()` looks up local-transit/candidate-peering findings by literal `source_asn`, but the table's `UNIQUE(kind, source_cc, target_asn)` constraint is keyed on *country*, not ASN. Two distinct upstream ASNs from the same country reaching the same target would pass the lookup (no match) then hit the raw constraint on insert -- an uncaught `sqlite3.IntegrityError`, swallowed by `run_batch()`'s per-corridor `except Exception`, silently leaving that corridor untested forever (refires a real Atlas measurement every night, no signal outside the gitignored log). Owner confirmed the intended identity semantics should be per-ASN (matching what `find_existing()` already assumes) -- the constraint needs to move to `UNIQUE(kind, source_asn, target_asn)` (with the nullable-`source_asn` detour case handled separately, since detours are legitimately keyed by economy not ASN). Not yet built; queued below.
  - Also flagged, not yet actioned: no automated tests anywhere in the repo (correctness rests entirely on the inline spot-verification already on record in this file); `_regenerate_artifacts()` failures log but don't block the commit, so a partially-failed regen could still get pushed; reverification can produce a second uncorrelated finding with no drift-detection when a corridor's classification changes since it was first filed; `outputs/reports/probe_gaps.txt` isn't in the regeneration list and will silently drift stale.
- **PF->Tokyo `detour_hub` correction** -- confirmed real (no traceroute ever showed an actual Tokyo exchange crossing for the 3 PF-sourced entries claiming it), blocked on the owner's direction: fix just these 3, or sweep the whole corpus for the same carrier-presence-not-measurement-confirmed pattern first.
- **Probe-to-probe geolocation anchoring** -- owner's idea, still fully undesigned.
- **RTT-based hop geolocation via multilateration, its own separate item (not folded into the probe-to-probe anchoring item above).** Owner's refinement: use the probe fleet's own known lat/lon plus each probe's measured RTT to a mystery hop to bound how far away it can physically be (RTT/2 x propagation speed through fiber), narrowing to a feasible region as more probes' RTTs are added as constraints -- the data (`min_rtt_ms`) is already captured on every hop of every traceroute this project has ever run, unused, in `data/atlas/parsed/*.json`, so no new measurements are needed to start. Concrete worked example the owner gave: testing OneQode's Guam facility hop from GU -- if every probe shows well under ~3ms RTT, that alone is strong evidence the hop is a genuinely local Guam router (3ms round-trip bounds the physical distance to something well within a single island, nowhere near a distant hub), independent of and corroborating the existing hostname/PTR evidence already in `regional_carrier_facilities.py`. Owner's own caveat, already agreed: precision will always be limited (cable routing isn't a straight line, propagation speed isn't a clean single constant for this region) -- output should be framed as a bounded plausibility check ("consistent with local," "inconsistent with local, must be remote"), not a precise coordinate. Not designed or built yet.

**Second caveat, more serious than imprecision, per the owner: MPLS label-anchoring can make a single-vantage-point RTT actively wrong, not just imprecise.** An MPLS LSR's ICMP TTL-exceeded reply can carry an address whose *identity* (hostname, registration, management-plane anchor) is tied to hardware in a completely different physical location than the router actually forwarding the packet -- the owner's own example: pinging Sydney from Brisbane, and the responding hop's RTT/identity reads like it's answering from Los Angeles, because the label itself is anchored to a US machine even though the physical forwarding happens in Sydney. A naive single-source RTT measurement can't tell this apart from a genuinely remote hop -- both look like "high RTT for a nearby-looking address." **This is why the design must fire from multiple, geographically diverse vantage points and check for *consistency*, not just take the multilateration bound from a single probe at face value**: a hop that's genuinely local shows a short, low-variance RTT from every probe that's actually nearby; a label-anchoring artifact tends to surface as a nonsensical or inconsistent pattern across vantage points (e.g., a "local-looking" address that even a geographically close probe measures as if it were distant) -- the inconsistency itself is the tell, not any single number. Folds directly into the existing "test more than one way" design requirement above; strengthens rather than replaces it. Not designed or built yet.

- **OneQode's second Guam facility** (Tata's Piti Cable Landing Station) -- known real, not yet added to `regional_carrier_facilities.py`.
- **`fetch_prefix_visibility`** -- built, never exercised against an actual question.
- **Probe-churn alerting** -- see the RESOLVED note above; adaptation is automatic, but nothing notices/flags a probe going offline mid-week.
- **UNIQUE-constraint / identity-key mismatch on `confirmed_local_transit`/`candidate_peering` findings -- RESOLVED (2026-09-17).** Replaced the single `UNIQUE(kind, source_cc, target_asn)` with two partial unique indexes: `idx_findings_identity_detour` (`kind, source_cc, target_asn` WHERE `kind = 'confirmed_detour'`) and `idx_findings_identity_asn` (`kind, source_asn, target_asn` WHERE `kind != 'confirmed_detour'`) -- `confirmed_detour` keeps its existing economy-keyed identity (it never records a literal source ASN), the other two kinds now match what `find_existing()` already looks up by. Migrating turned up a real pre-existing collision this bug had already caused, invisibly, in the legacy-migrated corpus: two `candidate_peering` rows for AS1221 (Telstra) -> AS140504 (Digicel Nauru) -- one sourced via AS24390/Fiji (recorded `source_cc: AU`), one via AS17828/PNG (recorded `source_cc: PG`, an old conflation of the *vantage point's* economy with the *upstream's*) -- had been filed as two separate findings only because the old schema deduped by economy, not ASN; `migrate_legacy_findings.py` would have merged them itself had it used the right key. Merged into one finding (both corroborations kept, `source_cc` corrected to AU -- Telstra's real registration country -- both original narratives preserved verbatim with a bridging note explaining the merge) directly in `findings_export.jsonl`, verified zero remaining `(kind, source_asn/cc, target_asn)` collisions across all 189 (down from 190) findings, then rebuilt the local gitignored `findings.db` from the corrected export via `import_jsonl()` (the project's own designed recovery path) under the new schema. Verified: `classify_loop_address`-style round-trip (re-export reproduces the corrected file exactly), and a direct test that two distinct-ASN same-country findings now insert cleanly while a genuine duplicate still raises `IntegrityError`. Regenerated all derived artifacts (`findings-export`, `corridor-backlog`, both reports, the geographic viz) -- `corridor_backlog.md` also picked up 2 new candidates (AS17828->AS141368, AS17828->AS152706) as a side effect of the tested-pairs cleanup from the escalations pass earlier this session, unrelated to this migration itself.
- **No automated tests for `auto_classify.py`/`store.py`** -- from the same review. The two historical-replay fixtures already used for manual spot-verification (GU->PG/AS17828, FM->PW/AS17893) are ready-made test cases; `run_batch()`'s lock scoping also deserves a concurrency test.
- **`_regenerate_artifacts()` failures don't block the commit** -- from the same review. Logs `logger.error` on a non-zero subprocess exit but never raises/signals failure, so `nightly_corridor_testing.sh`/`weekly_discovery_refresh.sh` can still commit+push a partially-regenerated, internally inconsistent set of artifacts.
- **Reverification can silently produce a second, uncorrelated finding when a corridor's classification changes since it was first filed** -- from the same review. No drift-detection/escalation when a reverified corridor now classifies as a different `kind` than its existing finding.

- **PeeringDB data-quality report section -- RESOLVED (2026-09-17).** Built: `on_peeringdb` tracked per-ASN (cached 30 days), new report section per economy. See the dedicated entry below for detail. Net-record staleness (`updated` timestamp) remains a deferred follow-up.
- **CSL (Samoa, AS38227/AS55579) is working on deploying a RIPE Atlas probe.** Project owner's own direct confirmation (2026-09-17), not derivable from any API -- Samoa (WS) is currently one of the confirmed genuinely-zero-probe economies (see the Pathway Coverage audit above). No action needed now; the weekly pipeline's own `asn_probe_registry` refresh will naturally pick it up once CSL's probe actually connects. Worth checking `outputs/reports/probe_pathways.txt`/the main report's Pathway Coverage table after that happens, to confirm WS moves off the zero-probe list.
- **Lesamoa.net (AS153053, Samoa) routing checked -- CORRECTED: satellite-like signature found on the real Apia-side address (2026-09-17).** Surfaced while adding the IXPs table's new Economies column: AS153053 is the WS member at **EdgeIX Auckland** (ix_id 4262, Auckland NZ, out-of-fishbowl -- confirmed via a live PeeringDB netixlan lookup, `103.180.190.63`). Free-source check first: AS153053's *only* RIS-observed neighbor is AS18400 (Xtreme Networks Limited, Wellington NZ WHOIS), 382/382 observations; its PeeringDB facility presence is "DataCentre220," Auckland NZ, not Samoa. Neither ASN carries bgp.tools' `satnet` tag.

  Fired a real traceroute to settle it properly (measurement 212437614, GU-sourced, 2/3 probes returned, project owner's explicit go-ahead given the Atlas-credit cost): both probes show the identical path, `AS3605 (Guam) -> ... -> 103.251.4.48 -> 180.222.193.85 -> 43.240.33.8/.7 -> 114.23.35.195 -> 203.171.32.160 -> 160.25.96.1 (target)`. PTR + WHOIS on every hop: `103.251.4.48`/`180.222.193.85` = **NextHop Pty Ltd** (`nexthop.net.au`, Sydney AU); `43.240.33.x`/`114.23.35.195` = **Voyager Internet Ltd** (`vygr.net`, Christchurch NZ); `203.171.32.160` = **Xtreme Networks** (`xtreme.net.nz`, Wellington -- the exact RIS-confirmed upstream). One large RTT jump (~180-210ms) occurs exactly at the Guam->Sydney leg on both probes -- consistent with ordinary long-haul submarine-cable distance, not a satellite signature -- and latency stays flat from Sydney through NZ to the target, with no second jump that a Samoa-bound satellite hop would produce. No satellite/VSAT/teleport naming anywhere in the path. **Conclusion: real terrestrial/submarine transit via Australia and New Zealand, no satellite hop detected.** Caveat: single source economy (Guam) so far -- a second vantage point from a different economy would further corroborate, not yet done.

  **Byproduct worth acting on**: this measurement also satisfies Validation Rule 1 on its own (RIS's only AS153053 neighbor, AS18400, is exactly the confirmed immediate upstream here) -- a legitimate `confirmed_detour` candidate (GU->WS, via Sydney/NZ) sitting unfiled in this measurement. Not filed yet; flagged for the project owner to decide whether to run it through the actual classifier rather than leaving it as session notes only.

  **Correction, same day, from the project owner's own `nmap -sP 160.25.96.0/23` scan**: revealed Lesamoa's real hostnames -- `link1-akl-wg`/`link1-apw-wg`/`link3-akl-wg`/`link3-apw-wg` etc. `akl` = Auckland, `apw` = Apia (Samoa's IATA airport code), `wg` = WireGuard -- at least 2 redundant encrypted tunnel links between an Auckland presence and an Apia presence. **The address tested above, `160.25.96.1`, is `link1-akl-wg` -- the Auckland *side* of the tunnel, not Samoa.** The "no satellite hop" conclusion is only accurate for that specific address, not for Lesamoa's actual Samoa-side network.

  Fired a second traceroute (measurement 212440030, same GU source for direct comparability) at `160.25.96.2` (`link1-apw-wg`, the real Apia-side address) instead. Both probes show the *identical* Guam->Sydney(NextHop)->NZ(Voyager)->Xtreme path as before, up to and including Xtreme's core router (`203.171.32.160`) -- then diverge sharply: an extra hop appears, `103.11.241.2` (`103-11-241-2.xtreme.net.nz`, WHOIS `descr: Xtreme Customer Addresses`, still AS18400), and the *single hop* from there to the target adds **+100ms (probe 64953) / +111ms (probe 60689)** round-trip -- versus only +9ms for the exact same final segment on the Auckland address. A ~100-110ms single-hop RTT jump landing right at a customer-facing edge, with no intervening hop, is a classic single-satellite-relay signature, and it's sharply different from the same network's own Auckland address showing nothing like it -- a clean differential comparison, not just one ambiguous number.

  **Revised conclusion: Lesamoa's Auckland-side infrastructure has no satellite hop (confirmed twice), but its actual Apia (Samoa)-side link shows a strong satellite-like RTT signature**, most plausibly Xtreme Networks providing VSAT/satellite backhaul specifically for that leg (a reasonable read of the evidence, not independently confirmed via Xtreme's own service documentation). Same single-source-economy caveat applies (both tests from Guam) -- a second vantage point would further corroborate. This is a good concrete illustration of why testing the *right* address matters: the first test was methodologically sound but aimed at the wrong side of a tunnel, and only the owner's own out-of-band packet capture caught it.

  **Project owner's own read (2026-09-17), domain expertise, not independently confirmed by this session**: suspects it's specifically a **Starlink** service carrying the WireGuard tunnels (rather than a traditional GEO/VSAT link) -- noted alongside, not in place of, the ~100-110ms RTT-jump evidence above, which the owner is better placed to interpret against real Starlink performance characteristics than a traceroute-only read. **Action item, queued**: once CSL's own Samoa probe (see the open item above) is actually connected, retest this corridor sourced *from Apia itself* -- a real local vantage point will settle this far more directly than an external traceroute ever can. Further out: the owner also floated cross-checking with independent GEO-location data later, which folds into this project's existing "RTT-based hop geolocation via multilateration" open item (see earlier in this file) rather than being a new, separate idea.

  **Conclusion for now, stated directly by the owner: AS153053 actually operates out of 220 Queen St, Auckland, NZ** -- not physically in Samoa at all, despite being a real, WS-registered ASN genuinely serving real Samoa customers (unlike the DNS-anycast exclusions elsewhere in this project, this is a legitimate local ISP with centralized infrastructure abroad, not a shell registration -- doesn't belong in `excluded_asns.py`). This independently corroborates, rather than merely repeats, this session's own earlier PeeringDB facility-presence finding ("DataCentre220," Auckland) -- two independent sources (the owner's own knowledge, and PeeringDB's facility record) agreeing on the same physical building. No code change made -- this project has no existing registry for "where an in-scope ASN's core equipment actually operates" (the opposite of `regional_carrier_facilities.py`, which tracks *external* carriers' facilities *inside* the fishbowl, not in-scope carriers operating *outside* it) -- recorded here as project knowledge only, pending the owner's direction if this pattern turns out to recur often enough to warrant its own tracked structure.

  **Business model check, per the owner's pointer to `lesamoa.net/about-us/` and `/services/` (fetched and read directly, not assumed)**: confirms Lesamoa is a managed-service-provider/reseller, not a pure network operator -- "the local Authorized Dealer for Fujifilm, Toshiba, Kaspersky, Fortinet, 3CX," IT/cabling/PBX/NVR services alongside their ISP division (opened 1996). Their own services page states **"We are connected through to the new High Speed Tui-Samoa Fibre (8Tbps),"** serving "Apia, Upolu to Salelologa, Savaii and between" -- no mention of Starlink, satellite, or VSAT anywhere on the site.

  **This is a real tension with the RTT evidence above, not a resolution either way -- recorded honestly rather than picked to fit one narrative.** The fibre claim describes their bulk customer-facing ISP service; it doesn't necessarily describe the specific `link1-apw-wg` WireGuard tunnel this project actually traced, which reads more like an internal/admin/out-of-band link between their Auckland office and Apia site (common practice: keep a secondary, transport-independent path to reach remote equipment if the primary link degrades) than customer traffic. The two could genuinely use different transport without contradiction. **Refinement for the planned Apia retest**: also traceroute toward one of Lesamoa's customer-facing addresses (e.g. `akl-gateway-customer.lesamoa.net`/`akl-gateway-customer2.lesamoa.net` from the same nmap scan, or a real customer prefix once identified) alongside the `apw-wg` tunnel address, to check whether ordinary customer traffic shows the same satellite-like signature or genuinely differs from the admin tunnel -- that comparison would do more to settle "fibre vs. satellite for whom" than retesting the same tunnel address alone.

  **Third traceroute fired same day** (measurement 212458784, GU-sourced again) at `160.25.96.94` (`akl-gateway-customer`) to start that comparison. Result: clean on both fully-contiguous probes -- final-hop increment into the target was only **+5.5ms** (probe 64953, ordinary terrestrial Guam path, same Xtreme Wellington core router as before) and **+8.3ms** (probe 65337, whose *own* path from Guam happens to transit SpaceX Starlink's `206.224.0.0/16` space -- confirmed via hop addresses, an accidental but useful cross-check that the satellite signature being looked for is about the *destination* segment, not just "a satellite hop somewhere in the path"). Both a world away from the ~100-110ms jump seen on the `apw-wg` tunnel.

  **But this doesn't actually settle the fibre-vs-satellite question for real Samoa customers -- it can't.** `akl-gateway-customer` is itself Auckland-labeled, per its own hostname; the nmap scan never surfaced an `apw`-labeled *customer* address, only the two `apw-wg` tunnel endpoints. So what this confirms is narrower than it first looks: Auckland-hosted infrastructure (both the admin tunnel and this customer gateway) is clean, full stop -- consistent with, not yet independent evidence for or against, the Tui-Samoa Fibre claim. The `apw-wg` tunnel remains the *only* Samoa-associated evidence either way, and it still shows the satellite-like signature. Genuinely settling this needs either a real Samoa-side customer address to test (none identified yet) or the planned retest from Apia itself once CSL's probe connects.

**Four routing-loop escalations from the 2026-09-17 01:xx nightly batch, investigated and resolved into `known_anomalies.py`.** All four were "unrecognized routing loop" escalations (same address answering at two consecutive TTLs, never a genuine multi-hop alternating loop) -- resolved by reusing the project's own `hop_investigation.py`/`ripestat.resolve_ip_to_asns` tooling to get each address's actual RIPEstat-announced prefix and holding ASN, rather than guessing:
- `184.104.192.252` (GU->TO, AS3605->AS38198) and `184.104.192.136` (GU->WF, AS3605->AS45879) both resolve to `184.104.0.0/15`, AS6939 (Hurricane Electric) -- their global backbone loopback range. Both traces continued past the loop to real subsequent hops rather than dead-ending.
- `209.120.142.174` (MP->PF, AS7131->AS55943) resolves to `209.120.128.0/17`, AS3257 (GTT Communications) -- seen immediately after a Cogent (AS174) hop, then a dead end, matching the already-documented "carrier-backbone dead-end is ordinary" pattern.
- `103.57.234.1` (MP->MH, AS7131->AS24439) resolves to `103.57.234.0/24`, AS7131 itself (WHOIS netname MTC-MP) -- the *source* carrier's own router. The same address shows up as an ordinary non-looping hop on GU(AS3605)-sourced corridors that transit it, and only doubled up on this one MP-sourced corridor.

Added all three as new `KnownLoopLocation` entries; verified `classify_loop_address()` now matches all four addresses and that none of the nine total entries' prefixes overlap. Cleared the four resolved entries from `escalations.md` (back to just the header). Also removed these four `(source_asn, target_asn)` pairs from the local, gitignored `data/analysis/tested_pairs.json` -- since they resulted in no filed finding, they aren't covered by the reverification queue either, so leaving them marked-tested would have stuck them permanently; the next scheduled nightly run will now naturally re-pick and reclassify them past the now-recognized loops.

**New corroboration source: Cloudflare Radar's RPKI ASPA data (2026-09-17).** Project owner's own find (bgp.tools/aspa/<asn> pages) -- an ASPA record (RFC 9582) is the AS holder's own cryptographically-signed statement of its legitimate upstream providers, a stronger signal than this project's existing RIS-neighbor-observation checks (which are inferred from BGP paths RIS happened to see, not an authoritative declaration). Checked every plausible free/bulk access path before building anything: bgp.tools and rpki-client's own public ASPA report are HTML-only, no documented bulk export; RIPEstat has no ASPA endpoint at all; RIPE NCC's RPKI Dashboard's ASPA management is account-holder-only, not a public read API. Cloudflare Radar's REST API (`/bgp/rpki/aspa/snapshot`) is the one genuinely scriptable option -- confirmed live, but it's the first authenticated third-party credential this project's discovery pipeline depends on (a Cloudflare API token scoped to Account > Radar > Read, project owner's own account).

Built `discovery/cloudflare_radar.py`, mirroring `bgp_tools.py`'s own conventions exactly: raw API response cached to one local file (`data/cloudflare_radar/aspa_snapshot.json`, gitignored under the existing `data/*` rule), refetched only when stale (24h window -- ASPA adoption is still early, records don't change often). Token loaded from `secrets.yaml` via a `load_cloudflare_api_token()` following the same shape as `atlas.secrets`/`discovery.secrets`.

**Caught a real bug against the live API, not just a guess**: Cloudflare's own API reference docs describe the response fields as snake_case (`aspa_objects`, `customer_asn`), but the live endpoint actually returns camelCase (`aspaObjects`, `customerAsn`) -- the first version silently parsed to an empty dict (`.get(..., [])` defaulting quietly rather than erroring) instead of raising, which would have looked like "this ASN just has no ASPA record" instead of "the parser is wrong." Caught by inspecting a raw response directly rather than trusting the docs. Fixed and verified against a real lookup: AS140504 (Digicel Nauru) -> `[12684, 45355, 132528]` (SES Astra, Digicel Fiji, Digicel Australia), an exact match with the bgp.tools page that started this -- two of those three are already this project's confirmed relationships, and AS45355 (Digicel Fiji) is a legitimate provider this project hasn't filed anything for yet. Also independently confirms the AS1221 (Telstra) `candidate_peering` finding merged earlier this session was correctly left unpromoted: AS1221 is absent from AS140504's ASPA list.

Not yet done, deliberately scoped out of this pass: wiring ASPA lookups into `auto_classify.py`'s classification rule order or into a promotion path for existing `candidate_peering` findings -- that's a real design decision (where in the rule order, how to treat "ASN not in ASPA list" vs "ASN has no ASPA record at all") worth its own discussion rather than folding in unprompted. For now this is a standalone, verified corroboration tool, same status `hop_investigation.py` has.

---

**ASPA wired into `auto_classify.py`'s promotion logic, 30-day recheck cadence (2026-09-17).** Project owner's explicit follow-up to the standalone tool above. Added two columns to `findings` (`aspa_confirmed`, `aspa_checked_at`) via the same additive-migration path as the UNIQUE-constraint fix -- schema edit, delete the local gitignored `findings.db`, reimport from `findings_export.jsonl` (which defaults both fields cleanly for all 189 existing rows, no collision risk this time since it's purely additive). `store.find_candidates_due_for_aspa_recheck()` finds `candidate_peering` findings never checked or checked 30+ days ago; `store.mark_aspa_checked()` promotes a confirmed one to `confirmed_local_transit` (an ASPA record is at least as strong a signal as this kind's usual RIS-agreement basis), appending an explanatory note rather than overwriting the finding's existing investigation narrative -- catches and gracefully downgrades an identity-collision case too (if an ASPA-confirmed upstream already has a *separate* `confirmed_local_transit` finding for the same target, logs a warning for a human look rather than raising or silently merging).

`auto_classify.recheck_aspa_candidates()` is the orchestrator (new `pacific-peering-aspa-recheck` console script), wired into `weekly_discovery_refresh.sh` alongside the existing reverify-enqueue step -- unlike Atlas-backed reverification this needs no account credits at all (one cached Cloudflare snapshot lookup), so it isn't rationed across nightly runs the way the reverification queue is; the 30-day gate exists only so a routine weekly call doesn't redundantly re-touch findings checked a few days ago.

Ran it for real against the live 19-candidate corpus (one fewer than 20 since the AS1221 merge): **zero promotions** -- verified this is a genuine result, not a bug, by manually checking all 19 candidates against the snapshot directly. Three of their targets do have published ASPA records (AS38198->[45355], AS55722->[7131, 14593], AS140504->[12684, 45355, 132528]) but none of the specific candidate upstream ASNs this project has on file match any of them -- ASPA adoption is still early, and none of these particular candidate relationships happen to be ASPA-confirmable yet. Every checked finding's `aspa_checked_at` still advanced, though, so re-ran the full regeneration once by hand to flush that into the committed `findings_export.jsonl` (a first version of `recheck_aspa_candidates()` only regenerated on `promoted > 0`, silently losing the checked_at stamps back to the gitignored local DB on a zero-promotion run -- fixed to always regenerate whenever anything was checked at all, promoted or not, before this ever ran unattended).

**ASPA progress report added, plus a candidate Validation Rule 7 proposal (2026-09-17).** Project owner's follow-up, three parts.

**1. New report section, both formats, before Visualizations.** `reports/data.py` gained `AspaEconomySummary`/`_compute_aspa_progress()`: per in-scope economy, how many of its own ASNs publish an ASPA record, and the distinct upstream ASNs those records declare, split by the report's existing in/out-of-fishbowl distinction (an in-fishbowl provider is itself one of this project's in-scope Pacific ASNs; out-of-fishbowl is an external carrier -- per the owner's own follow-up clarification, this needed to be two separate columns, not one combined count). One snapshot fetch total (not one per ASN -- the first draft called `get_aspa_providers()` per ASN, silently re-reading/re-parsing the cached JSON file ~160 times per report regen; refactored to fetch once and look up in memory). Real current numbers: 16 of 163 in-scope ASNs publish a record (3,104 total across the whole internet). Rendered in both `report.txt` and `report.html`, positioned identically (right after Candidate Peering, right before the viz section) so the two formats stay in lockstep per this module's own stated design principle.

**Made this resilient on purpose, since it wasn't before**: `_compute_aspa_progress()` now runs from `build_report_data()`, which `_regenerate_artifacts()` calls unattended on every nightly/weekly cron run. A missing token, an expired cache needing a live refetch, or a transient Cloudflare API error would otherwise take down the *entire* report generation over one optional section -- wrapped in a try/except that degrades to all-zero ASPA data and logs a warning instead, same posture `ris/ripestat.py` already takes for its own network calls. Verified directly (monkeypatched a forced failure): degrades cleanly, doesn't crash, real report files untouched by the test.

**2. TODO added for a "certified supply" marker**, per the owner's explicit request -- both report renderers now carry a TODO (ASCII: a comment near the new section; HTML: an HTML comment in the same spot) to eventually mark `confirmed_local_transit`/`candidate_peering` entries whose `aspa_confirmed` field is set, so an ASPA-backed confirmation reads as visually distinct from a RIS-agreement-only one. Not built yet -- explicitly scoped as a TODO, not an implementation, per the request.

**3. Candidate Validation Rule 7: ASPA absence as *positive* evidence for genuine peering, not just "unconfirmed."** Owner's own framing: "a lack of ASPA for peering [should] be correct" -- sideways traffic in a traceroute with neither RIS agreement nor an ASPA-listed provider relationship is itself a clue toward peering, not just a gap in evidence. The reasoning holds up: an ASPA record only ever declares a customer's *providers* (RFC 9582's whole purpose is upstream-authorization, not peer declaration) -- so a genuine settlement-free peering relationship would *never* appear in either party's ASPA record even once everyone has adopted it. That means, once a target *does* publish an ASPA record, its absence from that list stops being pure "absence of evidence" (this project's usual caution about RIS-invisible relationships) and becomes actual evidence *for* the peering hypothesis specifically -- the same category `CandidatePeering`/Validation Rule 4 already exists for (a clean, contiguous crossing RIS doesn't corroborate), now with a second independent source (ASPA) pointing the same direction instead of just RIS staying silent.

**Deliberately not built this pass** -- this is a real Validation Rule addition, and every prior one in this project (Rules 1-6) got reasoned through in prose here before any auto-promotion code touched it, precisely because Rule 1 (RIS + Atlas must *both* agree, no topology claim on one source alone) is this project's foundational credibility bar and has never bent for how compelling the circumstantial evidence looked (see: the AS3605->AS17893 GU-IX case, the clean single-traceroute-plus-real-exchange-membership cases, all kept as candidates on principle). Two concrete open questions before this becomes code, both needing the owner's direction rather than a guess:
- **Does ASPA-absence-plus-RIS-disagreement justify a new confirmed kind** (e.g. `confirmed_peering`, genuinely distinct from `confirmed_local_transit`'s customer-provider semantics), or does it stay inside `candidate_peering` with a stronger annotation/marker (tying into the "certified supply marker" TODO above)?
- **What precisely counts as "sideways" traffic** for this to apply automatically in `auto_classify.py` -- the existing candidate_peering rule (Validation Rule 4) already requires "clean, fully contiguous, RIS disagrees"; does the ASPA-absence check apply to every candidate meeting that bar already, or does it need its own additional shape criterion (e.g. both ends being in-fishbowl Pacific networks, which `CandidatePeering`'s docstring already implies is part of the existing shape)?

**Retracted, same day, per the owner's own correction.** The "absence as positive evidence for peering" half of Rule 7 above doesn't hold up -- it's the exact "absence of evidence is evidence of absence" fallacy this project already explicitly avoids elsewhere (RIS-invisible relationships, unannounced-but-real address space, etc.). The owner's precise correction: ASPA *presence* genuinely certifies upstream/provider status for the ASNs it lists -- and by the same token rules out a peering read for that specific pair, since ASPA only ever declares providers, never peers. That half is real, and already shipped (`recheck_aspa_candidates()`/`mark_aspa_checked()` promoting a `candidate_peering` finding to `confirmed_local_transit` when its upstream *is* ASPA-listed is exactly this in practice, correctly built). But ASPA *absence* proves nothing either way -- a target with no record, or one that simply hasn't listed a real peer (because peers were never ASPA's business to list), looks identical from this project's side to one where the candidate relationship genuinely isn't real. **Rule 7 is narrowed to just the presence direction, which is already built; no new "sideways traffic implies peering" logic will be added.** The two open questions above are moot and withdrawn along with it.

**New TODO, per the owner: a PeeringDB data-quality report section.** PeeringDB is incomplete for this project's purposes (known already from real cases on record -- e.g. AS12684/SES Astra and AS3549/the former Global Crossing backbone ASN both show zero registered facilities despite being real, active carriers this project has traceroute-confirmed) -- worth a dedicated report section quantifying that incompleteness directly (per in-scope ASN: has a PeeringDB net record at all, has a resolvable `net_id`, has any declared facility, has any declared IXP membership, staleness of last update, etc. -- exact metrics not yet specified by the owner, this needs its own short design pass before building, same as the ASPA section did). Not built this pass -- added to the open-items list only, per explicit request.

**PeeringDB data-quality section built + report restructuring, per the owner's follow-up (2026-09-17).** Two parts.

**1. PeeringDB caching (30 days) + the data-quality section.** `discovery/peeringdb.py`'s `fetch_net_ids` (renamed from `_fetch_net_ids`, made public) returns which ASNs have a PeeringDB `net` record at all -- previously computed internally by `fetch_facility_presence` and then discarded, conflating "not on PeeringDB" with "on PeeringDB, declared nothing" everywhere downstream. `analysis/fishbowl.py`'s `build_fishbowl()` now caches all three PeeringDB-sourced pulls (facility presence, IXP membership, on-PeeringDB status) to `data/peeringdb_cache.json` for 30 days, decoupled from the rest of the function's RIS-based fields which stay on the normal weekly cadence -- PeeringDB is the one source here this project has already been rate-limited by once, and its own data changes far less than RIS paths do. `fishbowl.json` gained a new `on_peeringdb: bool` field per ASN. Verified live: first run fetched fresh (76 of 163 in-scope ASNs on PeeringDB, 38 w/ IXP, 22 w/ facility -- matches a manual check done during design), second run reused the cache with zero live PeeringDB calls.

New "PeeringDB data quality" report section (both formats, right before ASPA progress): per-economy `ASNs | On PeeringDB | w/ Facility | w/ IXP`, the latter two now correctly denominated against *registered* ASNs rather than all in-scope ones. Reads straight from the already-cached `fishbowl.json` -- no live call, no graceful-degradation wrapper needed (unlike the ASPA section) since there's no network I/O in the report path at all now. Callout names the two known concrete cases (AS12684/SES Astra, AS3549) as real, traceroute-confirmed carriers PeeringDB itself has zero facility data for. TODO left for net-record staleness (`updated` timestamp) -- deferred, not in this pass.

**2. Report restructuring: pathway counts up front, full corridor detail collapsed.** Owner's request: the report's three "cards" sections (confirmed detours, confirmed local transit, candidate peering -- 157+13+19 = 189 entries, each with a full prose writeup) dominated the middle of the report. Added three new `ReportData` properties (`pathways_total`, `pathways_internal` = local-transit + candidate-peering since neither ever leaves the fishbowl by definition, `pathways_external` = confirmed-detour count, the "red box" number) and surfaced them as the first thing after the existing headline banner in both formats.

HTML: wrapped each of the three card sections in a native `<details class="section-toggle">` (collapsed by default, count shown in the `<summary>`) -- reusing the exact same disclosure idiom `_note_block()` already applies to individual long notes, just one level up. Verified well-formed (192 `<details>` open/close pairs matched, zero tag-mismatch errors via a full parse). ASCII can't truly collapse in a text format, so the three sections moved wholesale to a new "APPENDIX: FULL CORRIDOR DETAIL" section at the very end (after IXPS, before ABOUT) -- all 189 entries' full narrative kept verbatim, nothing deleted, just relocated so the report leads with structure and the plain counts rather than a wall of text. Verified: `Total pathways found: 189 / Internal: 32 / External (red box): 157`, appendix contains the same content that used to sit mid-report.

Regenerated everything (`findings-export` unchanged -- no finding data touched by this pass -- `corridor-backlog`, both reports, geographic viz).

**AS137064 (ISC F-root) excluded, DNS anycast formalized as a standing rule, "Low Data Quality Issues" appendix added (2026-09-17).** Project owner's sanity test: "ABQIX has a member from the Pacific, who?" -- fetched ABQIX's real PeeringDB member list directly (ix_id 3322, 12 members) and cross-checked every one against the in-scope registry. One hit: AS137064, registered PG. Cross-verified via three independent sources before concluding anything -- bgp.tools name ("ISC F-ROOT ABQ1"), PeeringDB's own net record (`aka: F-ROOT`), and RIPEstat WHOIS's authoritative `as-name: ISC-AS-POM1` / `descr: For use by ISC/F-Root exclusively for announcements at POM1/PNGIXP` -- all agreeing this is ISC's F-root DNS anycast infrastructure, not a genuine PNG network, its PG registration reflecting where ISC arranged BGP announcement for this instance, not real local presence. Swept all 163 then-in-scope ASNs' bgp.tools names and PeeringDB net names/akas for the same pattern before excluding just the one -- found and dismissed exactly one false positive (AS139609/SISCC, "isc" substring-matching inside "SISCC," a real, already-extensively-verified Solomon Islands carrier), no other genuine anycast instance.

**Excluded via the existing `discovery/excluded_asns.py` mechanism** (built for exactly this pattern already, from AS24013/DNS.SB) rather than inventing a new one -- added AS137064, and per the owner's explicit direction ("exclude all DNS anycast instances, as a rule"), formalized this as a standing rule in the module's own docstring rather than leaving it an implicit pattern across two one-off entries: any ASN confirmed to be root-server or public-resolver anycast infrastructure gets excluded under this same rule going forward, not re-litigated per instance. Added a `category` field to `ExcludedAsn` (`"dns_anycast"` on both current entries) so this is queryable, not just prose. Rebuilt the registry (163 -> 162 in-scope ASNs) and fishbowl (`on_peeringdb` 76->75, IXP membership 38->37, both losing exactly AS137064's own entries) -- confirmed no prior findings or tested-pairs history involved it, a clean exclusion.

**New "Low Data Quality Issues" appendix**, per the owner's explicit request ("as we find these issues, we should get a documented record of them"): `analysis/data_quality_issues.py` wraps `EXCLUDED_ASNS` into a generic `DataQualityIssue` record (title/category/description) rather than inventing a parallel data store -- single source of truth stays in `excluded_asns.py`, this module just makes it visible in the report. Deliberately left room for future non-ASN-exclusion-shaped issues (a PeeringDB record problem, an IXP misclassification, an ASPA discrepancy) via `_ADDITIONAL_ISSUES`, none yet. Rendered in both report formats as an appendix (HTML: right before the About footer; ASCII: right before the full-corridor-detail appendix) -- currently shows both AS24013 and AS137064 with their full verification narratives, not just a bare exclusion note.

Regenerated everything (`findings-export` unchanged -- AS137064 had no findings tied to it -- `corridor-backlog`, both reports, geographic viz). Verified HTML well-formed (zero tag-mismatch errors via full parse) after all changes.

**8 orphaned IXP registry entries pruned (ABQIX + 7 German exchanges), headline metric corrected (2026-09-17).** Direct follow-up to the AS137064/AS24013 exclusions: once those two DNS-anycast ASNs were removed from the ASN registry, checked whether any IXP entries in `ixp_lan_registry.json` existed *only* because of them. Live-checked every out-of-fishbowl entry's real PeeringDB membership against the current (post-exclusion) in-scope ASN set: exactly 8 had zero remaining in-scope members and aren't derivable via the country-code discovery path either (none of US/Germany are in-scope economies) -- ABQIX (AS137064's own artifact) plus DE-CIX Dusseldorf/Frankfurt/Hamburg/Munich, LOCIX Dusseldorf/Frankfurt, and MegaIX Dusseldorf (all AS24013's artifact -- matches that entry's own original flag, "Germany-only PeeringDB IXP records for a nominally Solomon Islands ASN"). No other orphans found among the remaining 23 entries.

Built `ixp_lan_registry.prune_orphaned_entries()` rather than hand-editing the JSON -- deliberately the *opposite* of `build_ixp_lan_registry()`'s own governance rule (existing entries are otherwise never dropped on a routine rebuild, since a human's in/out-of-fishbowl classification must never silently vanish); this is an explicit, separately-invoked action, run only when asked, matching `confirm_ixp_region`'s own pattern of being a deliberate call rather than automatic. Verified live against PeeringDB (not the possibly-stale cached fishbowl.json) before removing anything. Registry: 31 -> 23 exchanges (10 in-fishbowl unchanged, 21 -> 13 out-of-fishbowl).

**This moved the report's own flagship headline metric**: "% of real peering points sitting outside the fish bowl" dropped from **68% (21 of 31) to 57% (13 of 23)**. Not noise -- the prior figure was inflated by counting 8 "out-of-region exchanges" that were never real dependencies of any genuine Pacific carrier, only artifacts of two anycast DNS ASNs opportunistically registered under Pacific country codes. The corrected 57% is the first version of this headline number that reflects only real carrier behavior. Regenerated everything (findings-export unchanged, corridor-backlog, both reports, geographic viz) -- confirmed no committed finding data references any of the 8 pruned exchanges by name, so no cascading corrections needed elsewhere.
