**"Pathway coverage" report section added, right after IXPs (2026-09-17).** Direct follow-up to the "what pathways are missing" analysis: per economy, ASN count, subregion, total connected Atlas probes across its own ASNs, and how many of the other 19 in-scope economies still have zero finding connecting to it (`untested_pathways` -- deliberately not split by *why* it's untested in the table itself; that reasoning stays in conversation/task_plan history, the table just tracks the live count). Built from the same corrected economy-pair analysis as the ad hoc investigation (`_tested_economy_pairs_from_findings`, filtered to genuine cross-economy Pacific pairs, excluding self-pairs and external-carrier pairs) plus `atlas.asn_probes.load_asn_probe_registry()`. Verified the numbers reconcile exactly: summed `untested_pathways` across all 20 economies / 2 = 45 = 44 structurally-missing pairs + the 1 live NR-PG backlog candidate.

Confirms the same finding from the ad hoc analysis, now a durable, regenerated-every-time table rather than a one-off: Kiribati is the extreme outlier (17 untested, 0 active probes across 5 ASNs), and 8 more economies (AS, MH, NR, SB, TO, WF, WS, plus KI) have zero connected probes at all -- the real lever for closing most of the coverage gap is Atlas probe hosting, not more backlog cycles.

**New standalone report: probe-level pathway audit (2026-09-17).** Per the owner's request ("that list of probes and what pathways they cover"), following up on "35 unique probes" count. Built `reports/probe_pathway_report.py` (own console script, `pacific-peering-report-probe-pathways`, git-tracked output `outputs/reports/probe_pathways.txt`) -- same deliberately-standalone pattern as `probe_gap_report.py` (not wired into any cron script), since corroborations only ever record the source *ASN*, never which specific connected probe fired; got the missing granularity by cross-referencing each corroboration's `measurement_id` against the raw parsed Atlas file (`data/atlas/parsed/<id>.json`), which does record `probe_id`. Verified every measurement_id referenced by a finding has its raw file on disk (244 of 244).

**Caught and fixed a real bug before it shipped**: the first version's "N of 35 probes used" summary line counted pathways from 2 probe IDs that showed up in raw measurement files but are no longer in the *current* connected-probe registry (registry is refreshed periodically; these had gone stale/disconnected since being used) -- inflating the count from the correct 30 to a wrong 32. Fixed by computing the summary strictly against the current registry's own probe-ID set, and added a separate, clearly-labeled section for the 2 stale probes rather than silently dropping their history.

**Anomaly surfaced, not resolved solo, per standing practice**: one of the two stale probes (64391) shows 39 pathways spanning wildly different, unrelated source economies (NU, PF, PG, SB, US) -- unlike the other stale probe (60703), which cleanly shows only GU-sourced pathways matching a single ASN's expected footprint. Not explained by anything checked so far; flagged for the project owner rather than guessed at.

**Probe 64391 anomaly resolved: it was a bug in the new report's own code, not an Atlas data quality issue (2026-09-17).** Checked the RIPE Atlas API directly (`GET /api/v2/probes/64391/`): real, currently `status: Connected`, genuinely hosted at AS55885 (Telecom Niue LTD, NU) -- reconnected 2026-09-16, which is why it had dropped out of the periodically-refreshed `asn_probe_registry.json` snapshot (explains the "stale, not in current registry" classification, unrelated to the pathway-breadth question).

**The 39-pathway spread across unrelated economies was `probe_pathway_report.py`'s own labeling bug**, found by tracing one anomalous entry (a "SB -> SB" pathway) back to its actual finding: `_pathway_label()` used the finding's `source_cc`/`source_asn`, but for `confirmed_local_transit`/`candidate_peering` kinds those fields name the *upstream/provider* being tested, not where the traceroute originated -- the real origin is each corroboration's own `vantage_point_cc`. Verified this distinction matters here specifically (not just in theory): one corroboration of the SB(SISCC)<->Solomon-Islands finding has `vantage_point_cc: "NU"`, meaning that specific traceroute really was fired from Niue, via probe 64391 -- correct data, wrong field used to describe it. Confirmed `vantage_point_cc == source_cc` for all 198 `confirmed_detour` corroborations (so this never showed up as a problem there), but diverges for the other two kinds, whose whole point is that `source_*` means something else.

Fixed `_pathway_label()` to key off the corroboration's own `vantage_point_cc`/`vantage_point_asn` instead. Re-ran: all 39 of probe 64391's pathways now correctly show `NU` as the origin -- the anomaly is fully explained and gone, not hidden. Same fix naturally applies to every other probe's listing in the report, not just this one; none of the others happened to look anomalous before the fix only because their non-detour findings' upstream economy usually coincided with their real vantage point by chance.

---

**Probe registry refreshed live, full audit of "zero active probes" economies (2026-09-17).** Direct follow-up to the probe 64391 investigation: refreshed `asn_probe_registry.json` live against the current RIPE Atlas API (already wired into the weekly pipeline -- this was just a manual off-cycle run) and diffed against the stale cached copy. Exactly two changes, both explained: AS55885 (Niue, probe 64391) and AS152735 (a small Guam-registered ASN, "Guam Exchange", probe 60703) both reconnected 2026-09-16, one day after the last weekly refresh -- ordinary cadence lag, not a systemic problem. No other ASN's probe count changed.

**This answers "is NU not an issue on pathways?" directly: yes, corrected.** Niue's Pathway Coverage row now shows 1 active probe (was 0) -- a genuine, ASN-tracked local probe (AS55885, a real Niue ISP), not an artifact. Guam's count also moved 5->6 for the same reason, though Guam was never a zero-probe economy so this doesn't change any qualitative read.

**"Maybe we need a more extensive audit" -- done, and reassuring.** Cross-checked why `probe_gap_report.py`'s own live check ("15/20 economies have >=1 connected probe, 5 have none: AS, NR, SB, WF, WS") looked rosier than the Pathway Coverage table's 8-zero list (before NU's correction, 9). Not a bug in either -- they deliberately answer different questions. `probe_gap_report.py` counts *any* physically-connected probe in a country, for the country-based fallback-sourcing question; the Pathway Coverage table counts probes specifically on one of *this project's own tracked in-scope ASNs*, for genuine local-carrier ASN-based sourcing (the same thing `corridor_backlog.enumerate_candidate_corridors` requires). Checked every economy where the two disagreed, live, probe by probe:
- **KI (Kiribati), MH (Marshall Islands)**: their only connected probe(s) are hosted on AS14593 (SpaceX Starlink) -- already an established excluded/non-candidate ASN elsewhere in this project (satellite infrastructure, not a genuine local carrier). Correctly 0 in the Pathway Coverage table.
- **TO (Tonga)**: its one connected probe sits on AS24390 (University of the South Pacific -- a real institution, but registered under Fiji, not Tonga). Physically present in Tonga, not a genuine Tongan carrier network -- the same "physical location isn't network identity" principle already established for OneQode's Guam facility and the DNS-anycast exclusions. Correctly 0 for this table's purpose.
- **NR, AS, SB, WF, WS**: genuinely zero connected Atlas probes anywhere in the country, on any ASN. Nothing to reconcile.

No hidden data-quality problem found. The Pathway Coverage table is measuring exactly what it's supposed to; the apparent discrepancy with `probe_gap_report.py` was two different, both-legitimate questions, not an inconsistency. Regenerated everything with the refreshed registry (corridor-backlog, both main reports, both probe reports, geographic viz) -- `data/atlas/asn_probe_registry.json` itself stays local/gitignored, refreshed in place, nothing new to commit there.

**IPv6 coverage report section added, right after ASPA progress (2026-09-17).** Direct follow-up to the "how many probes support IPv6 / how many ASNs have IPv6 routes" question. Extended the existing IPv4 caching infrastructure rather than adding a live check to the report path: `ris/bulk.py` gained `fetch_ipv6_prefix_counts_for_registry()` (a lighter cousin of `fetch_aspaths_for_registry` -- just `fetch_originated_prefixes(af="v6")`'s count per ASN, no full BGP-state/neighbor data needed since this is adoption tracking only, not corridor testing), cached the same way and on the same weekly cadence as the existing IPv4 AS-path cache. `fishbowl.json` gained `num_ipv6_prefixes` per ASN. Probe IPv6 capability is checked live (one cheap batched Atlas `id__in` call for all tracked probes, same "cheap, live, no cache needed" posture `probe_gap_report.py` already uses for its own Atlas calls), wrapped to degrade gracefully rather than block the report on a network failure.

Real numbers: 47 of 162 in-scope ASNs (29%) have >=1 RIS-observed IPv6 prefix; of 37 connected probes, 9 work over IPv6, 2 more are IPv6-capable but tagged as not working. New Caledonia leads adoption (12 of 19 ASNs). Left a TODO in both formats for IPv6 corridor testing itself -- **per the project owner's explicit direction, deliberately deferred until adoption has advanced further**; this section is adoption visibility only, not a new testing capability.

**Listed vs. active probes, everywhere (2026-09-18).** Direct follow-up to the Kiribati audit, generalized by the owner: "we know there are many probes listed and not active" -- want both counts on the main report, and an actually-useful-probe breakdown on `probe_gaps.txt`, specifically so the finding "gives the community and ATLAS some[thing] to aim for: fix the data or get the probes back online."

Live-checked the shape of the gap first, country by country, unfiltered (`GET /probes/?country_code=X`, no status param): Samoa has 6 listed probes, 0 Connected (5 Abandoned, 1 Never Connected); American Samoa 2 listed, 0 Connected (Written Off + Abandoned); Nauru 2 listed, 0 Connected; Tonga 6 listed, 1 Connected; Fiji 19 listed, 1 Connected (the already-known Zscaler one); PNG 11 listed, 1 Connected. Solomon Islands and Wallis & Futuna have **zero probes ever registered**, not even abandoned ones -- a categorically different, more severe gap than the others, and the new reporting correctly leaves their probe breakdown blank rather than fabricating a "0 listed" line.

Built `atlas/probes.py::fetch_probes_for_economy()`/`build_probe_listing()`/`load_probe_listing()`/`asn_listed_registry()` as an unfiltered counterpart to the existing status=1-only `build_probe_coverage()`/`asn_probe_registry.json` -- deliberately additive, not a rewrite of the working active-only path (`pick_best_covered_economy` still needs active-only for real measurement sourcing). `PathwayCoverageSummary` gained `listed_probes` alongside `active_probes`; `_compute_pathway_coverage()` takes an optional `listed_registry` (falls back to the active one when absent, so it degrades safely before the listing has ever been fetched). Both `ascii_report.py` and `html_report.py`'s Pathway Coverage table gained a Listed column next to Active; verified HTML still well-formed via a full tag-balance parse (0 mismatches).

**Fiji's Zscaler probe reconnected off the VPN; audit chain uncovered a whole class of regional-org ASN mislabeling, ending in a source-selection architecture change (2026-09-19).** Started as a routine cron/probe-gap check, escalated through several real findings, each one prompting the owner's direct steer on what to do next.

*Zscaler note gone stale.* Rechecking Fiji's probe (60575) live against Atlas found it had moved off AS53813 (Zscaler) onto **AS141695 (Pacific Community)** on 2026-09-17, exactly the "hosting ASN changes off Zscaler" recovery `probe_gap_report.py`'s own `KNOWN_ISSUES["FJ"]` note had predicted. Removed the stale note (per the owner: "go with option 1, remove it").

*Domestic detours were structurally invisible, project-wide.* Owner: "we are doing a pacific peering project and not testing one of the most important issues: how effective is local peering?" and "the worst detours ... will involve FJ to elsewhere back to FJ." Checked the actual finding data: **0 of 160 confirmed detours were ever domestic**, anywhere, because `corridor_backlog.enumerate_candidate_corridors` excluded every same-economy pair unconditionally -- not a Fiji-specific gap, a project-wide blind spot for the exact failure mode this project exists to catch. Added opt-in `DOMESTIC_TEST_ECONOMIES` (starting with FJ).

*AS141695's own economy classification was wrong.* Building the domestic-FJ mechanism surfaced that AS141695 was tracked as **New Caledonia**, not Fiji, in `asn_registry.json` -- APNIC delegates it there (SPC's Nouméa HQ registration), but its probe live-geolocates to Suva and its own Atlas `description` reads verbatim "Pacific Community, Suva, Fiji"; both its RIS-observed neighbors are Fiji ASNs, none NC. Owner's call: "go with option 1, remove it" (the Zscaler note) then, once asked directly, "Reclassify to FJ." Relabeled the two `confirmed_detour` findings (FJ->KI via AS134783/AS154100) that had been fired from this probe under the NC label.

*A second, more dangerous case: AS24390.* Verifying the fix generally (checking every currently-tracked probe's live location against its registry economy, not just the one already found) surfaced **AS24390 (University of the South Pacific)**: tracked as Fiji, correctly for the org, but its connected probe (11691) live-geolocates to **Tonga** -- confirmed directly from the probe's own Atlas description, "USP Tonga Campus". This one mattered more: `run_smoketest` fires by Atlas *country* targeting, not the specific source ASN, so treating AS24390 as an FJ source would have silently fired from wherever Atlas's real FJ probe pool actually was while filing the resulting finding under AS24390/Fiji. Caught before it could fire for real: the corridor backlog was already at zero cross-economy candidates, so the first FJ-domestic candidate this mechanism generated (AS24390 -> AS4638) would have been first in line for that night's cron.

*Latency as a second validation signal.* Owner: "another test is latency, anything crossing the local IXP should be very low." Worked through the physics together (fiber RTT ~10 microseconds/km round-trip, confirmed against the owner's own figure) rather than fitting a statistic this project has no data to fit (zero recorded genuinely-local-IXP RTT samples exist yet). Landed on a **10ms threshold** as a physically-grounded engineering margin (Fiji's worst-case domestic span vs. an actual Sydney detour are separated by an order of magnitude, so precision within the threshold doesn't matter much) -- owner: "run with 10ms threshold, that is realistic for now, but note we may have to push that down when we have real data." Added `min_rtt_ms` to `traceroute_topology.py`'s `ixp_crossings` (the raw hop data already had it) and a new `auto_classify.py` escalation above `LOCAL_IXP_LATENCY_THRESHOLD_MS`, scoped correctly by construction (only fires on a hop matching a registered, non-"TBA" IXP LAN prefix -- confirmed against the actual registry, no ambiguous states).

*Quarantine, then a better architecture.* First built `atlas/probe_geo_audit.py`, a persisted per-probe audit (`data/atlas/probe_quarantine.json`) gating AS24390 out of candidacy -- a defensive fix. Owner pushed further: "I think we can trust the data on the probes before trying to classify them into the ASN's CC." Refactored `corridor_backlog.enumerate_candidate_corridors` to build candidate sources from `_probe_live_cc_by_asn` (grouping each ASN's connected probes by *live* economy) directly, superseding the quarantine gate with something better: AS24390 became a **correct Tonga source** instead of an excluded mislabeled-Fiji one -- Tonga had zero usable sources before this, so this unlocked 16 genuinely new cross-economy candidates, not just avoided a bug.

*Registry reclassification needed to survive regeneration.* A full pipeline re-run (wiring the geo-audit into `pacific-peering-pipeline` as its final step, the housekeeping the owner asked for) rebuilt `asn_registry.json` fresh from live APNIC data and **silently reverted the AS141695 fix back to NC** -- the earlier hand-edit was to a generated, gitignored file. Owner, asked directly whether target-side classification was still a live concern now that sources are probe-based: yes, in principle, but checked empirically against the one real case (AS24390's five existing target-side findings, all landing on the identical deterministic `144.120.0.1` from `pick_target_ip`'s always-first-prefix behavior) and confirmed correct (that address's own route-object `descr` reads "Laucala Bay Campus", USP's real Fiji HQ) -- not a bug, but worth checking again for the next multi-prefix target ASN, noted directly in `atlas/targets.py`. Added `discovery/reclassified_asns.py` (`RECLASSIFIED_ASNS`), applied last in `build_registry()`, scoped narrowly to AS141695 only -- unlike AS24390 (a genuinely multi-country network whose *core* identity is still correctly Fiji), AS141695's entire visible footprint agrees it's Fiji, not just one probe among several. Verified by rebuilding the registry fresh from live data and confirming the reclassification survives.

*Finding 25 retracted.* While investigating AS24390's historical usage, found finding 25 (FJ->VU via AS9249, a USP inter-campus corridor test) was fired from probe 60575 while it was *still on Zscaler* -- raw hop data showed private IPs then Zscaler-range addresses, meaning the AS-path narrative in its `legacy_note` doesn't reflect real network behavior. Owner: "retract it." Deleted the finding and its corroboration (cascades via the schema's `ON DELETE CASCADE`), and un-marked `(24390, 9249)` as tested so a genuine, correctly-attributed retest can happen.

Commits: `807370e` (AS141695 fix, domestic testing, Zscaler note removal, Local IXP Presence section), `fbb553d` (latency check), `508afe7` (probe geo-audit + quarantine), `0340817` (probe-live-cc source architecture), plus this session's closing commit (pipeline wiring, registry override, finding-25 retraction, this entry). Two new standing memory notes saved for future sessions: verify live probe geometry + raw hop data before mutating any finding, and the specific regional-org-ASN pattern (SPC/USP/Docomo Pacific) to watch for in future economies.

**The AS141695 "fix" was never actually fixed -- caught by running the very tests it unlocked (2026-09-19, same session, immediate follow-up).** Owner: "run all the FJ-FJ domestic tests now." Fired all 14 queued candidates; the first 6 to complete all resolved `AS53813` (Zscaler) as their literal first hop -- exactly the corporate-proxy pattern already retracted once for finding 25. The 2026-09-17 "reconnected off Zscaler" observation was real (probe 60575's *LAN-level* ASN metadata genuinely changed from 53813 to 141695), but the probe's actual *internet egress* never stopped routing through Zscaler's proxy -- a real, common enterprise setup (internal LAN on one network, internet egress proxied through a separate cloud security service) that a bare ASN-metadata check can't see. Owner's own framing, stated directly: "this really means we need to 'regression' test probes after their details change" -- exactly right, and now the standing lesson: a probe's metadata changing is a reason to verify its *next real path*, never a reason to assume a previously-known issue resolved itself.

Stopped the in-flight test run immediately (killed the background process) rather than let it burn more Atlas credits producing more identically-corrupted results, since Fiji's only live-verified probe is this same one. Re-verifying while investigating turned up a second, independent error, not just the new one: findings 195/196 (the two NC->KI detours "corrected" to FJ in the *previous* task_plan.md entry) were never actually sourced from AS141695's probe at all. Checked the real parsed measurement data (not assumed from the candidate label, the same mistake as before): both measurements' actual `probe_id`s (7018/33674/63050 on AS45345, 61210 on AS56089) are genuine New Caledonia probes. The `source_asn=141695` on that candidate was purely "which ASN just gained a connected probe, triggering this candidate's generation" -- never a claim about which probe fired the traceroute. Reverted 195/196 back to NC (their original, correct attribution) and retracted the 6 new Zscaler-tainted findings (197-202), with explicit confirmation from the owner before the deletion (the auto-mode classifier declined the combined revert+delete script as an irreversible-deletion action; split into an UPDATE-only pass plus a separately-confirmed DELETE). Un-marked all 6 retracted pairs as tested. Added AS141695 to `EXTERNAL_NON_CANDIDATE_ASNS` outright -- not just excluded from domestic candidacy like AS24390, since this ASN's *only* probe is unusable for path analysis regardless of which economy it's filed under. Fiji is back to zero usable sources, matching the pre-2026-09-19 pathway-coverage figures -- an accurate reflection of reality, not a regression.

Built the actual regression-test mechanism the owner asked for, not just a one-off fix: `auto_classify.py` gained a new classification rule 0 (`KNOWN_PROXY_ASNS`, ahead of the loop/IXP/RIS checks) -- any probe whose resolved AS-path starts with a known proxy ASN is dropped from classification and corroboration-writing (`clean_probes`, replacing every prior `triangulation["probes"]` reference in the function); if *every* probe in a measurement is proxy-corrupted, that files a genuine escalation instead of a target-IP retry (retrying a different destination can't fix a corrupted *source*). Validated against the six already-fired, already-retracted measurements by re-running `analyze_measurement()` on the cached data (no new Atlas credits) -- confirms the new rule would have caught the corruption on the very first corridor, not six findings later.

Also expanded domestic corridor testing from FJ-only to every economy with a real local IXP on PeeringDB (owner: "schedule all CC [with] an IXP next") -- `DOMESTIC_TEST_ECONOMIES` now covers FJ, GU, NC, PG, VU (recomputed live, not hand-guessed), unlocking 98 domestic candidates queued for the standing nightly batch rather than fired immediately.

**Overnight cron reviewed; found and fixed a real classification bug (false "proxy-corrupted" escalation), then a code-reviewer pass caught a livelock risk in the first version of the fix before it shipped; repo's first automated tests added (2026-09-20/21).** Routine review of the 2026-09-20 nightly corridor testing run (`e35adaa`, 2h budget): `{confirmed_local_transit: 19, inconclusive: 33, confirmed_detour: 26, candidate_peering: 6, escalated: 1}`. The 1 escalation -- `AS7131 (MP) -> AS24439 (MH)`, reverification of finding #77, reason "all probes proxy-corrupted" -- had a tell in its own detail string: an empty `()` where the proxy-report list should have been.

**Root cause, traced end to end, not guessed:** `data/atlas/{raw,parsed}/213457173.json` were both `[]` on disk, yet Atlas's own API (queried directly, hours later) showed the measurement was real and complete -- 1 probe (65653, genuinely-CNMI per the earlier probe-geo-audit entry above), 25 real hops, entirely inside AS7131's own address space, no proxy anywhere. `atlas/client.py::wait_for_results` polls until a measurement's status goes terminal, then fetches results *once*; Atlas's status and results endpoints aren't atomically consistent, so this measurement read "Stopped" moments before its results were actually indexed, and that single empty fetch got cached as the real answer. Downstream, `auto_classify.py`'s `if not clean_probes:` branch couldn't tell "zero probes reported anything at all" apart from "every probe's own path genuinely shows a proxy ASN" -- both produce an empty `clean_probes` list, so a pure infrastructure race got filed as a substantive network finding, and `mark_corridor_tested` permanently skipped a corridor that was never actually tested.

**First-pass fix** (before review): `wait_for_results` gained `empty_result_retries`/`empty_result_retry_delay` (retry a few times before accepting empty as real); `classify_corridor` gained an explicit `if not triangulation["probes"]:` check *before* the proxy loop, with its own "no probe data returned" reason, deliberately not marking the corridor tested. Un-marked `(7131, 24439)` in `tested_pairs.json` (it was never actually poisoned -- the bug fired via the reverify queue, which self-consumes on pick regardless of outcome, not via `tested_pairs.json`) and removed the false entry from `escalations.md`.

**Dispatched a `code-reviewer` subagent on that first-pass diff before calling it done** (per this project's standing agent-orchestration convention: code just written/modified gets reviewed). It found the fix itself correct in the narrow case but flagged real gaps, ranked by severity -- all addressed same session:
- **CRITICAL**: skipping `mark_corridor_tested` on a *persistent* zero-data corridor would let one `run_batch` worker re-pick the identical still-untested pair every time its source ASN freed up, burning the *entire* 2-hour nightly budget on one broken corridor instead of covering others (`_pick_next_for_batch`/`enumerate_candidate_corridors` don't consume a candidate on pick, unlike the reverify queue). Fixed with an in-run `skip_this_run: set[tuple[int,int]]` in `run_batch`, threaded into `_pick_next_for_batch` as a new `exclude_pairs` param, populated whenever a corridor escalates "no probe data returned" *or* raises an uncaught exception (the pre-existing blanket `except Exception` path had the identical shape).
- **HIGH**: the target-IP retry loop's zero-probes case still fell through to the alternate-prefix retry, doubling Atlas spend and -- confirmed against this exact historical case via `logs/cron.log` -- silently overwriting the first attempt's real (merely dark) data with the second, empty one, so only the info-free measurement ID ever reached the escalation. Fixed: `break` immediately on zero probes, before the `any_signal` check.
- **HIGH**: both early-return escalation branches wrote `escalations.md` (and, for the proxy branch, `tested_pairs.json` via `mark_corridor_tested`) *outside* `classify_corridor`'s own documented lock contract -- a real race under `run_batch`'s concurrent workers. Centralized through a new `_escalate_and_return` helper so both branches get the same lock discipline `_finalize()` already had.
- **HIGH**: `wait_for_results`'s retry-on-empty was wasted on the durable "No suitable probes" status (retrying can never produce results there) -- added `_NO_RETRY_STATUSES`, skipped entirely for that case.
- **HIGH, correctly flagged as pre-existing and out of scope, not fixed this pass**: `_fire_measurement` accepts a `target_ip` parameter and drops it -- `run_smoketest` always re-derives the *first* cached prefix regardless, meaning the "retry against an alternate prefix on a dead end" mechanism (added 2026-09-19 per the retry-on-alternate-IP feedback note) has never actually fired against a different address. Real, silent, and worth its own commit + test -- flagged to the owner rather than folded into this fix's scope.
- **MEDIUM** (all fixed): `_TERMINAL_STATUSES` was missing `"Failed"`/`"Archived"`; the retry loop had no tolerance for one transient HTTP error (now catches `requests.RequestException` and keeps retrying); `smoketest.py` now logs a warning when it persists a genuinely-empty result, so an empty cache file isn't silently indistinguishable from a real zero-probe measurement later.
- **LOW** (all fixed): an unreachable trailing `return` after the function's real return path; an `assert` used as control flow (now an explicit `if ... raise RuntimeError`); a stale docstring claim ("always marks tested, any outcome"); module docstring's rule list gained a "rule -1" entry for the new zero-data case.

**Repo's first automated tests, added this session** (`pytest` as a new dev dependency via `uv add --dev pytest`, `[tool.pytest.ini_options] testpaths = ["tests"]`): 9 tests across `tests/atlas/test_client.py` (retry-on-empty, exhausts-then-gives-up with an exact call-count assertion, "No suitable probes" skips retry entirely, one transient fetch error is tolerated) and `tests/analysis/test_auto_classify.py` (zero-probes escalates without marking tested; genuine all-proxy-corrupted still marks tested; zero probes does *not* fire a second measurement against an alternate target IP; `_pick_next_for_batch` correctly skips an excluded pair). Added `tests/conftest.py` with two autouse fixtures that replace `requests.get`/`requests.post` and `analysis.store.connect` with hard failures, so any future test that forgets to mock a call fails immediately instead of silently hitting the real Atlas API or writing into the real findings DB -- addresses the "no automated tests anywhere" item on the open-items list above, at least for this corner of the pipeline. `uv run pytest tests/ -v`: 9 passed. `ruff check`: clean on every touched file (the 9 pre-existing E402 warnings elsewhere are the same already-documented pattern, none in files touched this pass). Committed and pushed (`564d230`).

**Live retest of AS7131->AS24439 (finding #77's reverification), owner's explicit choice over waiting for tonight's cron.** Built the exact production `CorridorCandidate` via `_candidate_from_finding(conn, 77)` (not hand-constructed) and called `classify_corridor` directly. Result: a real, clean `inconclusive` (probes returned real data with a resolvable upstream this time, but it didn't meet any confirmed/candidate rule) -- no proxy-corruption mislabel, no escalation, corridor correctly marked tested via the normal path. `tested_pairs.json` confirms `(7131, 24439)` is tested again; `escalations.md`/`git status` confirm nothing spurious was written. End-to-end confirmation the fix works, not just the unit tests in isolation.

**The pre-existing `target_ip`-dropping bug the code review flagged above, fixed per the owner's explicit follow-up request (2026-09-21).** `run_smoketest` gained an optional `target_ip: str | None = None` param -- falls back to `pick_target_ip(target_asn)` exactly as before when not given, so `main()`/every other existing caller is unaffected -- and `auto_classify._fire_measurement` now actually passes its `target_ip` argument through instead of dropping it. Before this, every "retry against an alternate cached prefix" (the 2026-09-19 dead-end-retry mechanism, and this same session's own AS7131->AS24439 zero-probe investigation) was re-testing the identical first cached prefix under a log line that claimed otherwise.

Two new tests (`tests/atlas/test_smoketest.py`): `run_smoketest` uses a given `target_ip` without calling `pick_target_ip` at all (asserts via a `pick_target_ip` stub that raises if called), and falls back to `pick_target_ip`'s result when no `target_ip` is given. Plus one in `test_auto_classify.py` confirming `_fire_measurement` forwards its `target_ip` to `run_smoketest`. All three would have failed against the pre-fix code (old `run_smoketest` had no `target_ip` parameter at all -- calling with one raised `TypeError`). `uv run pytest tests/ -v`: 12 passed (up from 9). `ruff check`: clean. Committed and pushed (`d4c662d`).

**Overnight cron reviewed (nothing to report); picked up the queued Starlink-backhaul TODO; mined the 8 pre-existing Guam Starlink-probe corroborations for free source-side data (2026-09-23).** Routine review of the 2026-09-22 nightly run (`784298a`): 0 corridors tested (backlog genuinely empty, `pacific-peering-corridor-backlog` confirms 0 candidates), no new `escalations.md` entries. Nothing to report, so picked up the open item queued 2026-09-20 (line ~2974 above): study how Starlink (AS14593) itself backhauls traffic into the region, distinct from the already-decided exclusion of its 4 hosted probes from local-carrier candidacy.

**Owner asked first what Starlink corridors even look like and where AS14593 peers/procures transit** -- answered from real data before designing anything new. `findings.db` corroboration chains already show the *downstream* (target) view (many sources' traceroutes transiting AS14593 en route to Kiribati/Tuvalu). Checking for the *source* (outbound) view turned up something not previously noticed: **8 existing GU-sourced findings (50, 54, 133, 226, 227, 228, 230, 231) already carry AS14593's own outbound path for free** -- Atlas's country-based probe selection for Guam has been incidentally including probe 65337 (hosted on AS14593) as one of the 2-3 probes fired in ordinary corridor tests, all along. No new Atlas credits needed to see it; just had to pull probe 65337's raw hop data (not the abbreviated `chain` summary) out of the 14 measurements behind those 8 findings.

**Mined all 14, every hop IP resolved live via RIPEstat `network-info` (not assumed from the `chain` field or from memory):**

- **Consistent shape**: dishy/router/CGNAT private hops (`10.0.0.1`, `192.168.1.1`, `100.64.0.1`) -> AS14593's own routed gateway range (`206.224.64.0/21`, confirmed live -- more precise than the earlier /16 note) -> one of two immediate handoff ASNs -> real transit carriers -> destination.
- **7 of 8 hand off through AS137074** ("RISE-AS-AP", `103.43.212.0/22`); the 8th (finding 226, GU-domestic) hands off through **AS21859 (Zenlayer, `129.227.176.0/23`)** instead. AS137074 has no PeeringDB net record and exactly one RIS-visible BGP neighbour (AS63927, RISE-HK) -- looks like a thin regional-transit AS Starlink leases into RISE's Hong Kong/APAC fabric rather than a carrier in its own right, though that's inference from the visible shape, not confirmed from RISE's side.
- **Downstream of the handoff, five distinct real transit carriers appear**, each verified by resolving the actual hop IPs: AS3257 (GTT), AS7578 (GSL/Global Secure Layer), AS9299 (PLDT, Philippines), AS2497 (IIJ, Japan), AS6939 (Hurricane Electric), plus AS4637 (Telstra Global) further downstream in two chains.

**The RTT split is the real finding: Starlink's own segment is fast; the terrestrial handoff carrier is where the latency lives.** Every corridor's jump from the last private hop to AS14593's own gateway is ~40-60ms total (one LEO hop plus first-mile terrestrial backhaul). The large jumps (+60-250ms) all happen *downstream* of Starlink's handoff, inside the transit carrier's own network:
- **GU->PG** (50): 65ms at AS3257 entry -> 214ms crossing into AS4637 (Telstra Global) -- never reaches AS17828 cleanly, dead-ends on a repeated Starlink-range address (`206.224.77.98`).
- **GU->PW** (54): 69ms at AS7578 entry -> 154ms reaching AS17893's own address (`103.159.28.1`) -- the entire jump happens inside GSL's own network (`206.148.24.0/22`, the same block reused in finding 228).
- **GU->SB** (133): 65ms at AS3257 entry -> 202ms crossing into AS4637 -- dead-ends at `103.142.98.131`, **the exact address already on record in `known_anomalies.py` as AS139609 (SISCC)'s own loop location** (2026-09-18 entry above) -- independent corroboration from a completely different, previously-excluded probe.
- **GU domestic, AS17456->AS3605** (226): the one corridor routed via AS21859/Zenlayer -> AS9299 (PLDT) -- **a Guam-to-Guam destination detours through the Philippines** before landing on Guam Cablevision's own network at the final hop. Smaller jump (45ms->107ms) but a genuine detour by this project's own definition -- just never filed as one, since AS14593 is source-excluded.
- **GU domestic, AS3605->AS17456** (227): via AS3257 (GTT) then AS2497 (IIJ, Japan) -- another Guam-to-Guam corridor leaving the region, this time via Japan, before IIJ hands off directly into AS3605's network.
- **GU->GU AS9246/Teleguam** (228): entirely inside GSL's network, 54ms->119ms, no international carrier crossing visible in the resolved hops.
- **MP(AS7131)->GU AS23676/MARIIX** (230) and **->AS56200** (231): both via AS7578 -> AS6939 (Hurricane Electric), 62-65ms->106-317ms. Finding 231's last resolved hop (`154.18.76.1`) is the identical address flagged as an "unrecognized routing loop" from probe 60689 in this same finding's other corroboration (`escalations.md`, 2026-09-19) -- probe 65337 reaches it cleanly (no loop, just goes dark after), reinforcing that it's ordinary AS7131-internal space, not an anomaly.

Two addresses stayed genuinely unresolved (`210.173.173.82`, `101.203.90.137`/`.153`) -- no announced prefix found via RIPEstat for either; left as an honest gap, not guessed.

**Where AS14593 peers, cross-checked against PeeringDB live** (not just traceroute inference): 108 IX presences worldwide; the Pacific/APAC-relevant ones are EdgeIX + MegaIX Sydney, AKL-IX + MegaIX Auckland, MegaIX/VIC-IX Melbourne, WA-IX Perth, EdgeIX/MegaIX Brisbane, JPIX + Equinix + Telehouse Tokyo, Equinix + Global Switch + Science Park Singapore, GetaFIX + BBIX Manila, DE-CIX + Extreme IX Mumbai. Facility data shows the APAC gateway footprint concentrates hard at **Sydney, Tokyo, Singapore** -- matching the "Sydney"/"Tokyo" `detour_hub` labels this project has already been recording on Starlink-adjacent findings (100, 138, 176, plus four of the eight mined here) from traceroute inference alone, now with structural PeeringDB confirmation.

**Not filed as findings** -- observational only, since AS14593 stays deliberately source-excluded from candidacy (`EXTERNAL_NON_CANDIDATE_ASNS`), and none of this required a new probe selection or Atlas credit. This entry is the record; no code or schema change.

**Open question for the owner:** two of the eight (226, 227) show real domestic detours (Guam traffic leaving the region via the Philippines/Japan) that `DOMESTIC_TEST_ECONOMIES` would normally flag and file -- but never will here, since the *source* is the excluded Starlink probe rather than a real local ISP. Worth deciding whether "Starlink's own domestic routing" deserves a parallel, clearly-labeled structural table, or stays out of scope as a satellite operator's own backhaul choice rather than something a real Guam ISP does.

**Kiribati (1008228/1008229) and Marshall Islands (64237) still have zero data** -- confirmed this session that neither economy has ever had a corroboration recorded at all (KI/MH never get selected as a corridor source under current candidacy rules, since their only probes sit on the excluded AS14593), so seeing their outbound Starlink paths requires firing new measurements, unlike Guam's free ride. Destination set and firing plan for those still need to be designed with the owner before spending any Atlas credit.

**Owner's domain correction on the CGNAT hops seen in the Guam mining pass: Starlink is IPv6-first internally, so the current all-IPv4 measurements are structurally blind to its real routing (2026-09-23).** Every one of the 14 measurements mined above starts with the same private-address run (`10.0.0.1`, `192.168.1.1`, `100.64.0.1`) before reaching AS14593's own routed range -- read at the time as "dishy/router/CGNAT", correctly, but not pursued further. Owner's point, not independently verified by this session but consistent with Starlink's known architecture and taken as credible per the project's standing trust in the owner's network-engineering background: those CGNAT hops are an **IPv4-only artifact**. Starlink's internal network runs IPv6-first, so an IPv6 traceroute over the same corridor would very plausibly skip the NAT layer entirely and show Starlink's actual internal path, not a translated one.

`atlas/client.py::create_traceroute_measurement` hardcodes `"af": 4` -- every traceroute this project has ever fired, Starlink or otherwise, is IPv4-only. Filed as a TODO in the same place this project already tracks deferred IPv6 work (`reports/ascii_report.py`'s and `reports/html_report.py`'s existing "IPv6 corridor testing" TODO, from the 2026-09-17 coverage-report entry above and the 2026-09-16 owner directive at line ~2452) rather than a new, disconnected note -- this is a concrete instance of exactly the general IPv6-testing gap already queued there, not a separate idea. **Not implemented** -- IPv6 corridor testing stays deliberately deferred project-wide per the owner's own earlier call; this is scoped as a TODO for when that work starts, specifically flagging that Starlink corridors will need `af=6` to be read correctly, not just any IPv6 support in general. Verified: both report generators still `py_compile` clean, regenerated `report.txt`/`report.html` (diff is timestamp/live-ASPA-count drift only, no structural change), no code behavior changed.

---

**KI/MH Starlink-probe-sourced measurements designed, fired, and mined -- IPv4 for now, per the owner's explicit call to proceed despite the CGNAT gap above (2026-09-23).** Unlike Guam, Kiribati and Marshall Islands never get free incidental data (confirmed last entry: neither economy has ever been selected as a corridor source), so this needed new measurements. Design, approved before firing: source by explicit probe ID rather than country -- the only way to pin a measurement to these specific Starlink-hosted probes -- toward each economy's own most heavily-externally-corroborated domestic target, to test the same question the Guam data couldn't answer (none of Guam's 8 mined chains happened to be domestic-to-a-heavily-tested-target): does a probe *physically inside* the country still see its own domestic traffic bounce out to a distant hub the way every external source into that target already does?

**Implementation**: added `atlas/smoketest.py::run_probe_sourced_traceroute(probe_ids, target_asn, target_ip=None)` -- the first function in this module to source by `source_type="probes"` rather than `"country"`/`"asn"`, reusing the existing `_fire_and_persist` helper exactly like `run_ixp_member_probe` does for its own source type; `client.py` needed no changes, already generic on `source_type`. Two new tests in `tests/atlas/test_smoketest.py` (explicit multi-probe sourcing produces the correct comma-joined `source_value`; a given `target_ip` is used without re-deriving via `pick_target_ip`), matching the existing `run_smoketest` test pattern. `py_compile`/`ruff check` clean, `uv run pytest tests/ -v`: 14 passed (up from 12). Committed and pushed (`a497434`) before firing anything, per standard practice.

**Fired two measurements** (both probe-sourced, single-shot, real Atlas credits, not filed as findings -- same "observation only" posture as the Guam mining pass, since AS14593 stays source-excluded from candidacy):
- **KI, both probes together** (`1008228,1008229`) -> AS154100 (BNL Tarawa's own address, `202.1.22.1`) -- the most exhaustively externally-corroborated KI target in the whole project (9+ independent chains from GU/MP/VU/PF/CK/PW/FJ/TV/NC), never before sourced from inside Kiribati. Measurement 214562743.
- **MH, single probe** (`64237`) -> AS24439 (`103.202.149.1`) -- 11 existing external `confirmed_detour` findings, same logic. Measurement 214563597.

**KI result: both probes die entirely inside Starlink's own private/CGNAT space, never reaching AS14593's own routed range at all.** Probe 1008228 resolves 4 hops (`192.168.1.1` -> `100.64.0.1` (49.5ms) -> `172.16.251.0` -> `172.16.251.115`, 44.6ms) then goes fully dark; probe 1008229 the same shape (`192.168.2.1` -> * -> `172.16.250.122` -> `172.16.250.13`, 45.2ms) then dark. Neither probe ever reaches `206.224.64.0/21` (AS14593's own confirmed gateway range from the Guam mining pass), let alone a downstream carrier -- a **materially different, weaker result than every Guam chain**, where the private hops always resolved into that gateway range by hop 5-6. This isn't the "address-specific dead end" shape the standing retry-a-different-prefix policy targets (`atlas.targets.list_target_ips`) -- the packets never leave Starlink's own network on either probe, so a different target IP for the same ASN almost certainly wouldn't change anything. Genuinely unclear whether this is rate-limiting/ICMP filtering specific to these two probes, or a real structural difference in how Kiribati's Starlink backhaul responds to traceroute -- **flagging to the owner rather than guessing**, per standing practice for anomalies this session can't confidently resolve on its own.

**MH result: reaches AS14593's own range, then hands off directly to AS6453 (Tata Communications) -- no intermediate ASN, unlike every Guam chain.** `192.168.1.1` -> `100.64.0.1` (53.8ms) -> `172.16.250.10` (52.4ms) -> `206.224.70.194`/`206.224.70.178` (AS14593, `206.224.64.0/21`, confirmed live via RIPEstat, 53-57ms) -> `216.6.52.2` (AS6453, 56.2ms) -> `216.6.54.3` (AS6453, 85.4ms -- the real jump, +29ms) -> `180.87.180.57`/`209.58.61.40`/`180.87.180.33` (all AS6453, 85-93ms plateau) -> dark. Two things worth noting: (1) **no AS137074/AS21859-style regional-transit hop appears here** -- Starlink hands MH's traffic straight to Tata rather than through the RISE/Zenlayer layer seen on every Guam chain, a real difference in handoff architecture, not just a different carrier name; (2) the trace dies inside AS6453's own network before reaching AS24439, but **this independently corroborates the existing data rather than contradicting it** -- 8 of AS24439's 11 existing externally-sourced findings already carry `detour_hub="Los Angeles"`, and AS6453 (Tata Communications America) is exactly the kind of carrier with major US-hub presence that label implies.

**Not filed as findings** (same reasoning as the Guam mining pass); raw + parsed JSON persisted under `data/atlas/{raw,parsed}/{214562743,214563597}.json`. No code or schema change from the firing itself -- only the new `run_probe_sourced_traceroute` function, already committed separately above.

**Open question for the owner:** the KI dead-end is the one piece of this that isn't just "interesting data" -- it's a real gap in what this investigation can see. Worth deciding whether to retry with a different KI-hosted probe pairing target (unlikely to help, per the reasoning above), accept that Kiribati's Starlink outbound path is currently opaque from this vantage and move on, or treat the dead-end itself as the finding (Starlink's Kiribati ground segment behaves differently under traceroute than its Guam or Marshall Islands segments do).

**DNS-anchor TODO implemented, not fired yet (2026-09-24).** Added `atlas/starlink_anchors.py` (`pacific-peering-starlink-anchors`): finds Connected AS14593 probes in the nightly-refreshed `data/atlas/probe_listing.json` rather than hardcoding IDs, then fires one traceroute per anchor (`1.1.1.1`, `8.8.8.8`) with all probes in each. **Per-anchor column added to the weekly IPv6 report; baseline rebuilt with it (2026-09-24).** Owner-approved. Each IPv6 probe's snapshot now carries `anchors`: per service (`cloudflare`/`google`, keyed by service so a fallback-address week still compares), whether it reached, the anchor's own RTT, and the last hop that answered. The report shows `ok <RTT>` / `dark@<addr>` / `-` per service. The weekly diff adds "now reaches / no longer reaches the <service> anchor", and older snapshots without `anchors` are tolerated. The committed baseline was rebuilt from the cached results of 215118287/215121164, with no new measurements. Access values and economy counts are identical to the original, and the rebuild checked they match. **AS150349 mislabel fixed: SISCC's "Samoa downstream" was a transcription error (2026-09-24).** Owner spotted AS139609 (SISCC) listing WS as a dependent economy, knowing that's not possible. Traced it to one legacy finding, `NU -> WS (AS150349)`, filed 2026-09-16 by hand (`confirmed_detours.py`, `findings.db` row 164), with `target_cc="WS"` and a note calling it "a real, if unusual, cross-region relationship (a Samoan ISP...)". **AS150349 (Pacific Vaizeds Enterprise Ltd, PVEL-AS-AP) is a Solomon Islands network.** APNIC `country: SB`, `MAINT-PVEL-SB`, last-modified 2022-10-05, so it was never Samoan. The owner supplied its aut-num, whose export policy declares `to AS139609 announce AS150349`: the operator itself names SISCC as its upstream, independently corroborating the adjacency. `asn_registry.json` already had it under SB, and the later `TO -> SB (AS150349)` finding (row 250) was correct.

Fixed: row 164 `target_cc` WS→SB and note rewritten (the correction is recorded inline in `legacy_note`). The legacy dataclass entry was corrected to match so a re-migration can't bring it back. DB backed up to the session scratchpad before the update. Export and reports regenerated. **SISCC's dependents are now SB only, and it drops out of the concentration table.** The other report changes are live-data drift since the 09-23 build (ASPA 16→18, IPv6 probe count, PG/FM coverage columns), not this fix.

Swept all 243 findings' economy labels against the registry. 30 mismatches: 13 external carriers (out of scope by design), 13 AS24390/USP-Tonga-probe sources (documented), 2 AS141695 sources (settled 2026-09-19: fired from genuine NC probes). That leaves **one more genuine candidate: `NU -> SB (AS136996)`**. APNIC registers AS136996 (Pacific Networks, PACIFICNETWORKS-AS-AP) to **VU**, and it was hand-filed on the same day (`confirmed_detours.py:3665`). **Not changed; asked the owner** whether Pacific Networks has any real SB presence. New regression test `tests/analysis/test_findings_economy_labels.py` checks every finding's labels against the registry, listing documented exceptions explicitly and AS136996 as pending (the test fails if a pending entry goes stale). Verified: the test failed on the stale export and passes after the re-export; 44 pass.

Also corrected: today's earlier entries called AS9471 "Vini"; it is **ONATI** (RIPEstat holder ONATI-AS-AP, matching the rest of this file).

**PF Google IPv6 dead end rechecked: persistent, and not specific to Google DNS (2026-09-24).** Owner asked to check it now. Three measurements from both ONATI (AS9471) probes: **215190120** (`2001:4860:4860::8888`, repeat), **215190566** (`2001:4860:4860::8844`, Google's secondary DNS), **215191707** (`2001:4860:4826:7700::`, www.google.com's AAAA, resolved via 1.1.1.1 from this host, in a different /48 of AS15169's `2001:4860::/32`). **All 6 traces are identical:** hop 1 = the shared gateway (`2405:cc00:3020:3185:3e37:12ff:fecf:880a`), hop 2 silent, hop 3 = `fc00:1::19`, hop 4 = `fc00:1::1`, then silence until Atlas's gap limit. None reached its target. The morning baseline had the same shape, so it held for hours.
- **Both PF probes are one vantage point:** same /64 (`2405:cc00:3020:3185::/64`, inside AS9471's `2405:cc00:3000::/36`), same gateway. Their agreement is one observation, not two.
- **The split is at hop 4, just after the shared ULA router `fc00:1::19`.** Cloudflare-bound traffic goes to `2402:6d00::52`, a public address in **AS55943** (ONATI's other ASN, `2402:6d00::/32`), and reaches the anchor at ~1.5ms. Google-bound traffic goes to a second ULA-numbered router, `fc00:1::1`, and nothing answers beyond it.
- *Inference, not verified:* ONATI's internal routing sends Google's IPv6 to a different next hop (`fc00:1::1`) than Cloudflare's. Past that hop, either IPv6 toward Google is broken, or every later hop and Google itself are silent to ICMP from here. The second is less likely: the same Google anchors answered ICMP traceroutes from 7 other probes this morning.
- **Not tested:** Google's other IPv6 space (e.g. `2404:6800::/32`); IPv4 to Google from these probes (would separate IPv6-only from all-Google); a non-ICMP probe. Flagged for the owner.

**Owner's assessment of the PF Google IPv6 dead end: ONATI is blocking it (2026-09-24).** Owner's read, domain expertise: since the trace goes to a ULA, ONATI must be blocking Google IPv6. Recorded as the working explanation. For precision: the working Cloudflare path also crosses a ULA hop (`fc00:1::19`), so the evidence is not ULA addressing alone. It's that Google-bound traffic *alone* is sent to a different ULA next hop (`fc00:1::1`), with nothing answering beyond it, toward all three Google IPv6 targets, persisting for hours. That is consistent with a deliberate policy block or null route inside AS9471. No further measurements planned. The weekly IPv6 fleet check now watches it for free: while blocked it stays `google dark@fc00:1::1` with no diff line; if ONATI lifts the block, the next Wednesday report prints "probe 53098/1001562 (PF) now reaches the google anchor".

**Cloudflare ROV test built; weekly on Wednesdays, baseline firing now (2026-09-24).** Owner's idea: Cloudflare announces RPKI-valid and RPKI-invalid test prefixes; trace both from every probe and see where the invalid traces stop. Owner's choices: baseline now plus weekly; simple labels of our own rather than `rpki_audit`'s vocabulary. `rpki_audit` (a separate repo) already runs a paired valid/invalid comparison and records the off-path pitfall; this reuses the idea, not its code.
- **Targets** (A/AAAA of valid/invalid.rpki.cloudflare.com today, second address as fallback): v4 valid `104.17.230.6` (`104.17.224.0/20`, AS13335, RPKI valid), v4 invalid `103.21.244.8` (`103.21.244.0/24`, invalid: AS0 ROA on the /23); v6 valid `2606:4700::6811:e606` (`2606:4700::/44`, valid), v6 invalid `2606:4700:7000::6715:f408` (`2606:4700:7000::/48`, invalid: AS0 ROA). Each run re-checks all 8 via RIPEstat (new `ris.ripestat.fetch_rpki_status`) and **won't fire** if any is no longer in its expected state.
- **Per probe per family:** `not_filtered` (invalid answered), `filtered` (valid answered, invalid didn't; `drop_asn` = last ASN the invalid trace reached, i.e. that network or its upstream dropped the route; None = died before the first public hop), `inconclusive` (missing result, valid unreached, or path starting at a `KNOWN_PROXY_ASNS` proxy). AS paths come from the existing `resolve_traceroute_hops`/`extract_as_sequence`.
- **Caveat recorded in the report:** Cloudflare is anycast, so a short path (e.g. NC's local Cloudflare) only speaks for the probe's own network.
- Output: `outputs/reports/rov_cloudflare.txt` (per economy, per drop ASN, per probe, week-over-week changes) and `rov_cloudflare_history.jsonl`. Wednesday block of the nightly script, after the IPv6 check, same `|| echo` failure tolerance. Plan: IPv4 37 probes, IPv6 11, 4 measurements. `smoketest.refetch_parsed` was factored out of `ipv6_fleet` for both to use. Verified: 9 new tests, 53 pass, ruff clean, `bash -n` clean, a live dry run passes the RPKI target check.

**First ROV baseline attempt stopped: measurement description too long (2026-09-24).** The description listed every probe ID; with 37 IPv4 probes it passed Atlas's 255-character limit, so Atlas refused both IPv4 measurements (nothing created, no credits). The IPv6-valid measurement (**215197526**, 11 probes) was created before the run was stopped. It was stopped deliberately so it wouldn't save a baseline with every IPv4 result `inconclusive`. Fix: `starlink_anchors._fire_anchor` falls back to a probe count when the ID list makes the description too long (Atlas records the probe list itself), and `create_traceroute_measurement` now rejects a 255+ character description before posting. 2 new tests, 55 pass. 215197526 is left unused; the re-run fires IPv6-valid again.

**Cloudflare ROV baseline recorded (2026-09-24).** Measurements **215197667**/**215198886** (IPv4 valid/invalid, 37 probes) and **215199848**/**215200172** (IPv6 valid/invalid, 11 probes). None needed a fallback. Holder names below come from RIPEstat.
- **New Caledonia is the only economy where every conclusive result is filtered:** IPv4 5/5, IPv6 3/3 (63050's IPv6 is inconclusive). Invalid traces from AS45345 (Nautile) probes 7018/33674 die at **AS18200 (OPT-NC)**. 1008625/63050 (also AS45345) and 61210 (AS56089, OFFRATEL, downstream of AS18200) die at their own edge. All consistent with OPT-NC dropping the invalid route. *Inference:* a traceroute can't separate AS18200 filtering from AS18200 lacking the route from its own upstream.
- **TO (USP's probe 11691, AS24390):** filtered. The invalid trace dies inside AS24390, and its valid path runs `24390 -> 7575 (AARNet) -> 13335`. Consistent with AARNet filtering upstream; not verified.
- **GU:** 8 not_filtered, 1 filtered: probe 23039 (AS17456, Pacific Data Systems) dies at its private first hop, while its valid path goes via AS3605, whose own probes are not_filtered. GU 7385's IPv6 is filtered@local too.
- **PG 50365 (AS17828, PNG DataCo):** not_filtered, but the **only path split in the set**. Valid goes via **AS4826 (Vocus)**, invalid via **AS7474 (Optus)** into Cloudflare. That fits Vocus dropping the invalid route and PNG DataCo falling back to Optus, which carries it. Inferred from path selection.
- **Not filtered anywhere on the path:** PF (7/7 IPv4, 2/2 IPv6), FM, PW, VU, TV, NU, MP, KI, and every Starlink (AS14593) probe on IPv4.
- **Inconclusive:** CK 22761 and MH 64237 (no results), FJ 60575 (Zscaler proxy, caught by the proxy check), KI IPv6 x2 and NC 63050 IPv6 (probes without working IPv6).
- **Classifier fix made while checking the baseline:** PG was first labelled `filtered@AS13335`, but its invalid trace had reached Cloudflare's own network, which means the path carried the route. An invalid trace reaching AS13335 now counts as `not_filtered`. Each verdict also records `diverged`, where the valid and invalid AS paths first differ (the PG signal). The baseline was rebuilt from refetched results (no new measurements); only PG's label changed. 2 new tests, 57 pass.
- **Flagged, not acted on:** FM probe 62046 is listed on AS14593 (Starlink), but both its traces start in AS139759 (FSM Telecom).

**ROV section added to the main reports, right after ASPA (owner, 2026-09-24).** `report.txt`/`report.html` gain "ROV enforcement (Cloudflare RPKI-invalid test, weekly)" between ASPA progress and IPv6 coverage. It shows filtered / not-filtered / inconclusive probe counts per economy for IPv4 and IPv6, where invalid traces died, any valid/invalid upstream splits (currently PG: Vocus vs Optus), and which economies are untestable (no connected probe: AS, NR, SB, WF, WS). The intro frames it against ASPA: ASPA counts who has *published* route-security data, and this shows whose paths *enforce* origin validation. It keeps both caveats (filtering vs upstream filtering; anycast short paths). Data comes from the latest line of `rov_cloudflare_history.jsonl` via new `reports/rov_data.py`, kept out of `reports/data.py`, which is already past the file-size limit. `ReportData` gains an optional `rov` field. Nightly script: the Wednesday block now regenerates both reports after the weekly checks, because the batch only regenerates them on nights it tests something, so the section would otherwise lag. Verified: 3 new tests (`tests/reports/`), 60 pass, touched files ruff-clean (the 7 pre-existing ruff errors in other `reports/` files are unchanged), `bash -n` clean, and both reports regenerated with section order ASPA → ROV → IPv6.

Fetched it: "a 100% Vanuatu owned private company based in Port Vila, Vanuatu", one office (Rue de Dauphine, Nambatu Area, Port Vila), no other country mentioned. APNIC also says VU. `findings.db` row 155 corrected from `NU -> SB (AS136996)` to `NU -> VU`, with the correction recorded in `legacy_note`; the legacy dataclass entry was corrected to match. DB backed up to the session scratchpad first. Also recorded: its sole RIS neighbor AS136557 is Host Universal Pty Ltd, registered in Australia, so this is a Vanuatu network whose only observed upstream is an Australian hosting carrier. Removed from the economy-label test's pending list; the test failed on the stale export and passes after re-export. Export and reports regenerated. **The 243-finding economy sweep now has no undocumented mismatches.**

That's a third independent source for Vanuatu, after the aut-num country and the company website; the registered address differs from the website's Nambatu office, but both are in Port Vila. **AS136996 (Pacific Networks) is a Starlink reseller; its traced address appears to sit in Australia (2026-09-24).** Owner noted its website says it resells Starlink. Fetched pacificnetworks.net: "Vanuatu's First Starlink Authorized Reseller"; also sells VPS hosting, dedicated servers and colocation. Re-read the hops of the existing finding's measurement **212158127** (NU 64391 → `103.101.192.1`): Cogent hop 10 at 285.7ms, AS136557 (Host Universal, AU) hops 11–12 at 285.9/285.5ms, the **target itself at 285.3ms: no RTT increase after the Australian hop.** A Vanuatu endpoint behind Starlink should add roughly 40–60ms beyond the Australian hop. *Inference, not verified:* `103.101.192.1` terminates at Host Universal in Australia, i.e. Pacific Networks hosts its IP space/services in AU, with Vanuatu customers carried over Starlink (possibly tunnelled). Same general shape as the owner's earlier Samoa read (line ~2738). The finding's routing facts stand; what it shows is where AS136996's address space lives, not a path into Vanuatu. **Proposed check, not fired:** one traceroute from a few AU probes to `103.101.192.1`; a few ms would confirm it's hosted in Australia.

**AS136996 hosting check: its address space is in Sydney; reaching it from Vanuatu takes an unexplained long detour (2026-09-24).** Owner-approved measurements to `103.101.192.1`:
- **AU, 215206130** (10 requested, 9 returned): probe 31347 (AS137576, northern Sydney) reaches the target in **0.825ms**, right behind AS136557's router (0.849ms). RTT rises with distance from Sydney: Sydney/Central Coast 1–9ms, Canberra 7ms, Adelaide ~20ms, Brisbane 23ms, Perth 49–58ms. Every probe's last hop before the target is AS136557. **The address is hosted in Sydney** (not at Host Universal's registered Adelaide address).
- **VU, 215206410** (AS9249 probes 64783, 1008536, both returned): `AS9249 (1–2ms) -> AS38442 Vodafone Fiji (14–16ms, then 50–55ms) -> AS136557 at 173ms / 240ms -> target 170ms / 241ms`. **A +120–190ms jump between Vodafone Fiji and Host Universal with no hops shown**, even though Host Universal is <1ms from Sydney. *Inference, not verified:* hidden MPLS/tunnel hops, or a handoff outside Sydney, possibly in the US; the NU-sourced trace (212158127) also crossed Cogent at 125ms before reaching Sydney at 285ms. Flagged for the owner.
- Conclusion recorded on finding row 155 (re-exported): Pacific Networks, a Starlink reseller, hosts this address space in Sydney; the finding shows where AS136996's space lives, not a path into Vanuatu. **Open for the owner:** whether AS136996 should still count as an in-scope Vanuatu corridor target, given its announced space lives in Australia.

**AS136996 (Pacific Networks) excluded and its finding removed (owner, 2026-09-24): "remove it, it's contaminating actual data."** Its announced space is hosted in Sydney (measurements 215206130/215206410 above), so any corridor "into" it measures a path to an Australian host, not into Vanuatu.
- Added to `discovery/excluded_asns.py` under a **new category, `offshore_hosted`**: a genuine local company whose announced address space is hosted outside its economy. The existing standing rule only covered DNS anycast. The evidence is in the entry's note. It now appears automatically in the report's data-quality appendix (3 exclusions).
- **Removed** findings.db row 155 (`NU -> VU (AS136996)`) and its corroboration row 200 (measurement 212158127), in one transaction, after checking the IDs matched. Backups of the DB and the prior export are in the session scratchpad. The legacy `confirmed_detours.py` entry was replaced by a comment so a re-migration can't bring it back. No other finding referenced AS136996.
- Rebuilt `asn_registry.json` (162 → 161 in-scope ASNs; VU 12 → 11) and the fishbowl dataset (161 entries, AS136996 gone; it also picked up a week of fresh RIS data ahead of Sunday's refresh). Re-exported findings (243 → 242), regenerated both reports and the corridor backlog (0 candidates). Report totals all read 161; total pathways 243 → 242; Sydney hub 109 → 108.
- **Kept deliberately:** `tested_pairs.json` history (AS136996 can no longer generate candidates), and the two hosting-check measurements as the exclusion's evidence. 60 tests pass.

**Offshore-hosting sweep over cached traceroutes: no further candidates, but coverage is thin (2026-09-24).** Owner asked whether other ASNs like AS136996 could be found. Physics test, free, cached data only: for every trace that reached an in-scope target, Δ = target RTT minus the RTT at the **first** hop of the last non-economy ASN before it. Compare with the minimum fibre round trip from the finding's hub to the economy's capital (great-circle km ÷ 100 km/ms, coordinates from `discovery/economy_coordinates.py`). Flag when every trace has Δ ≤ 5ms and the minimum is ≥ 15ms.
- **Positive control:** AS136996 (deleted finding, re-injected from cached measurements 212158127/215206410) is caught in all 3 traces (Δ −3.5..0.4ms vs ≥ 24.7ms needed).
- **First-pass false positive, fixed:** measuring from the *last* foreign hop flagged AS38227 (CSL Samoa) and AS38800 (Digicel Samoa). Their upstream AS132528 is DIGICEL (AUS) PTY LTD, registered in AU but a regional backbone (also upstream of AS45355 Digicel Fiji, AS140504). Inside AS132528 there is a +49ms jump between `45.127.173.29` (hub) and `103.73.110.38` in both traces, above the 43ms Sydney→Apia minimum. So its last router is in Samoa and the targets are genuine. Measuring from the first hop of that ASN's run fixes it.
- **Result: no other in-scope ASN is flagged.** **Coverage is the limit:** only 22 of 161 target ASNs are checkable, since the rest never answered in a cached trace or their findings have no hub label. Absence of a flag is weak evidence for the other 139.
- Not built into the pipeline; the scratch script lives in the session scratchpad. **Extending coverage needs measurements:** e.g. RTT from a few hub-country probes (AU/US/JP) to one address per in-scope ASN, where a few ms from a hub means hosted there (the AS136996 AU check, generalised).

**Offshore-hosting check built: nightly self-scheduling, full sweep monthly plus new ASNs; first full run firing now (owner, 2026-09-24).** Generalises the AS136996 test. New `atlas/offshore_check.py` (`pacific-peering-offshore-check`) pings up to 3 addresses per in-scope ASN (one per originated prefix) from AU 3 / NZ 2 / US 3 / JP 2 probes. An address is `offshore` if some probe's RTT is below 0.7 × the fibre minimum from *that probe's own location* to the economy's capital (great-circle km ÷ 100 km/ms). The 0.7 margin allows for capital-vs-island distance and coordinate slop. Other verdicts: `consistent`, `no_response`, `not_measured`; an ASN is offshore if any of its addresses is. **Flags are never auto-excluded:** each new one is appended to `escalations.md` for the owner, and the report is `outputs/reports/offshore_check.txt` + `offshore_check_history.jsonl`. Raw ping results are cached so a run can be re-judged for free. **Schedule (owner):** runs every night from the nightly script after the probe-gap report; a full sweep if the last full run is ≥30 days old, otherwise only registry ASNs never checked before, otherwise nothing. Uses ping rather than traceroute (the test only needs minimum RTT). New `client.create_ping_measurement` takes several probe specs in one measurement; the traceroute creator's pre-post checks moved into a shared `_validate_definition`. Credits: balance 101.2M; a full run is about 265 measurements × ~10 results, a few thousand credits. Plan: 161 ASNs, 265 addresses, 38 untestable (originate no prefix, so nothing to contaminate). Verified: 10 new tests (8 in `test_offshore_check.py`, including the real AS136996 0.825ms-from-Sydney case as the positive control; 2 for the ping creator), 70 pass, ruff clean, `bash -n` clean.

**First full offshore-check run stopped: hit Atlas's 100-concurrent-measurement cap (2026-09-24).** The first version fired its batches back to back. After ~103 created, Atlas refused the rest ("You are not permitted to run more than 100 concurrent measurements"). Stopped before anything was recorded: no history, report or escalation written. That mattered because it would have logged a "full" run with ~160 `not_measured` addresses and blocked retries for 30 days. (Side note: my first `pkill` pattern matched its own shell command and killed that shell; re-checked with a non-matching command: process gone, nothing written.) The ~103 orphaned pings still ran on Atlas (a few hundred credits). **Fixed:** each batch of 20 is finished (polled to a terminal status) before the next fires; a concurrency-cap refusal waits 60s and retries up to 5 times; a full run with any `not_measured` address is saved as `complete: false` and doesn't count as the monthly run; an ASN counts as checked only once actually measured. 5 new tests, 75 pass.

**TODO, queued (owner, 2026-09-24): Atlas rate-limit / concurrency pre-flight check, shared by every job that fires measurements.** Today's two refusal classes, the per-target 25-concurrent cap (Cloudflare/Google anchors) and the per-account 100-concurrent cap (offshore check), were each found only by being refused mid-run. Scope: one helper in `atlas/client.py` that, before a job fires, reads the account's current in-flight count (`/api/v2/measurements/my/?status=1` Scheduled and `status=2` Ongoing; checked today, 99 ongoing + 4 scheduled from the stopped run), and optionally the credit balance and daily spend (`/api/v2/credits/`). Before starting, a job would wait or back off until headroom exists for its batch. Callers: `auto_classify.run_batch` (up to 5 concurrent), the Wednesday IPv6/ROV steps, and `offshore_check._measure`. That matters most on nights when several of these run back to back. Not built yet.

**Atlas rate-limit pre-flight check built (the TODO above, owner asked to build it now, 2026-09-24).** New `atlas/rate_limit.py`. `count_in_flight()` makes one `/measurements/my/?status__in=0,1,2` call; it agreed with the per-status sum when checked live. `wait_for_headroom(n)` blocks until `n` more fit under 100 − 5 (Atlas's account cap minus a margin for another job starting in between), polls every 60s, and gives up after 30 min, letting the caller fire and rely on its refusal handling. It **fails open**: if the count can't be read it logs and proceeds, so a flaky stats endpoint can't stall the nightly run. `credit_status()` reads balance and daily spend and warns below 100k credits. **Wired in at the two points every measurement passes through:** `smoketest._fire_and_persist` covers all traceroutes (the nightly corridor batch's concurrent workers, the Starlink/IPv6-fleet/ROV steps, one-off checks), and `offshore_check._measure` checks each ping batch. The per-target 25-concurrent cap stays with the anchor fallback. Verified: 6 new tests with fake counters (no network), the offshore batching test updated to assert a pre-flight per batch, 81 pass. Live: 20 in flight during the running offshore check, balance 101.2M, room for 20 → True. (The offshore run already in progress started before this change and keeps its own in-memory batching; this is used from its next run.)

**Offshore check sped up: batches of 80, 4-minute batch wait (2026-09-24).** The retry run was correct (no refusals) but only 40/265 addresses after ~30 min: one-off pings from four countries sit "Ongoing" for minutes while a few probes never report, so each 20-batch took ~10 min, over 2h in total. With the pre-flight guarding the account cap, `BATCH_SIZE` is now 80 (80 + 5 nightly workers + 5 margin < 100) and each batch waits at most 240s. Expected full run: ~20–25 min. The slow run was stopped by exact PID (python 2971414, found via its parent chain; not a name pattern this time) before recording anything. Its ~40 pings cost a few hundred credits.

**Correction: the "stop stragglers" change was based on a wrong diagnosis, and is reverted (2026-09-24).** One-off pings are **not** stuck for 20+ minutes. Measurement 215224701 started at 1790250601 and stopped *by itself* at 1790250960 (359s): Atlas closes one-off pings about 6 minutes after start. The "77 in flight" was batch 1 still inside that window, not stragglers. A manual stop attempt raced them: Atlas answered "That measurement cannot be stopped" because they had just closed on their own. So stopping early buys nothing and would log a 400 per measurement per batch; reverted as `3379c5e` (`git revert`, history kept). Real per-batch cost: create (~1–2 min) + ≤4 min wait + the remaining ~2 min until Atlas closes the batch, then the pre-flight lets the next one fire. About 8 min per batch, ~30 min for a full run. **Also fixed:** an ASN announcing a covering prefix and a /24 inside it produced the same `.1` address twice (5 duplicate pings in batch 1; no two different ASNs share an address, so no verdicts affected). Addresses are now de-duplicated per ASN before taking up to 3. 1 new test, 82 pass.

**First full offshore-hosting check: 4 ASNs flagged, 3 look real, 1 likely a probe-location error (2026-09-24).** 265 addresses (after the per-ASN de-dup), 0 refusals, 4 batches of 80 with the pre-flight holding each batch until Atlas had room. **Result: 4 offshore, 71 consistent, 48 no response, 0 not measured, 38 untestable** (no originated prefix). The run is `complete`, so it counts as this month's full run. All 4 were escalated to `escalations.md`. **None excluded; owner to decide.** Each flag checked after the run (probe locations, prefix origin via RIPEstat):
- **AS10131 Telecom Cook Islands (CK), `202.65.33.0/24` only:** 4.3ms from a southwest-Sydney probe (1004726), rising with distance (Brisbane 18ms, Auckland 31ms, US/JP ~190ms). Physical minimum Sydney→Rarotonga ~50ms. **Hosted in Sydney.** Its other prefix `202.65.32.1` is consistent (in CK). Partial: one prefix, likely a Sydney PoP/infrastructure block of the national incumbent. Suggests a *per-prefix* exclusion from targeting, not an ASN exclusion. Corridor targeting already uses `list_target_ips[0]` = `202.65.32.1`.
- **AS23959 Owl Limited (VU), `194.127.166.0/24`:** 11.2ms and 13.2ms from two Tokyo probes, everything else ≥113ms. **Hosted in/near Tokyo.** RIPE-region `194.x` space; its other address `194.114.136.1` didn't answer. Strongest candidate for an AS136996-style exclusion; worth checking what Owl Limited is.
- **AS132468 SATSOL (SB), `103.188.182.0/23`:** 11.1/13.7ms from Sydney probes, Melbourne 15.7ms, Auckland 26.7ms; physical minimum Sydney→Honiara ~28.5ms. **Hosted in Sydney.** SATSOL is a satellite ISP, consistent with a Sydney teleport/gateway. Its `103.115.80.1` is consistent and is the address existing SISCC↔AS132468 findings were measured against. Partial, like AS10131.
- **AS58932 PMCI Palau Mobile (PW), `103.30.248.1`: likely a false positive.** The only probe to beat physics is 1000112, registered as an "LA VM" in Los Angeles but on **AS3258 xTom Japan**. It answered in 48.7ms, the same as a Tokyo probe (48.4ms, plausible for Palau). The likeliest explanation is that the probe's registered location is wrong. Unconfirmed. Implies a robustness rule: require ≥2 probes from different ASNs to beat physics before flagging.
- Follow-ups for the owner: per-prefix vs per-ASN exclusion for AS10131/AS132468; what Owl Limited (AS23959) is; whether to add the ≥2-independent-probes rule. Also, a pre-flight refinement: fire the part of a batch that fits instead of waiting for room for all 80 (batch 3 waited ~4 min behind 22 slow pings).
